On Separation Logic, Computational Independence, and Pseudorandomness (Extended Version)
Ugo Dal Lago, Davide Davoli, Bruce M. Kapron

Abstract
Separation logic is a substructural logic which has proved to have numerous and fruitful applications to the verification of programs working on dynamic data structures. Recently, Barthe, Hsu and Liao have proposed a new way of giving semantics to separation logic formulas in which separating conjunction is interpreted in terms of probabilistic independence. The latter is taken in its exact form, i.e., two events are independent if and only if the joint probability is the product of the probabilities of the two events. There is indeed a literature on weaker notions of independence which are computational in nature, i.e. independence holds only against efficient adversaries and modulo a negligible probability of success. The aim of this work is to explore the nature of computational independence in a cryptographic scenario, in view of the aforementioned advances in separation logic. We show on the one hand that the semantics of separation logic can be adapted so as to account for complexity bounded adversaries, and on the other hand that the obtained logical system is useful for writing simple and compact proofs of standard cryptographic results in which the adversary remains hidden. Remarkably, this allows for a fruitful interplay between independence and pseudorandomness, itself a crucial notion in cryptography.



1
Introduction

Cryptographic primitives and protocols can be defined and analyzed using two
distinct models, i.e. the
computational

model
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
and the

symbolic
model
[
4
,
5
]
.
While in the latter adversaries have unlimited computational power,
in the former they are assumed to be
efficient (i.e. polytime) randomized algorithms. In the last forty years,
research concerning the interactions between logic and cryptography has been intense,
but while the symbolic model, given its more qualitative
nature, has been the object of many systematic
investigations
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
, the same
cannot be said about the computational model, in relation to which the
proposals for logical systems are more scattered and mostly recent (see
Section
9
for a more in-depth discussion on related work).

Proposals concerning logical systems that capture
computational

cryptographic
reasoning are bound to face, at least in principle, some
challenges. The first is certainly the fact
that adversaries have limited complexity, and can have a non-null, albeit
negligible, probability of success. All that must be somehow reflected in
the logical languages used, which are often not standard. Similarly, the
underlying notion of equivalence between programs cannot be the standard, exact,
one, but must be relaxed to so-called computational indistinguishability, the
logical nature of which has been studied from different viewpoints
[
11
,
12
]
. Finally,
one should mention pseudorandomness, the natural computational counterpart to
randomness, whose logical status has recently been scrutinized from the perspective
of descriptive complexity
[
13
]
.

There is yet another aspect of the computational model which is deeply rooted in
probability theory, and which is known to be fruitful in cryptography. Since
the work by Shannon, indeed, it is well known that the perfect security of a
symmetric cipher can be formulated very elegantly as the
independence
between
the random variable modeling the message and the one modeling the ciphertext. On the one
hand, it is certainly worth noting that since Yao’s pioneering work on the
computational model, strong links are known between the notion of
computational security for symmetric encryption schemes and appropriate variations of the
concept of independence
[
3
]
, all this thus generalizing Shannon’s definition to the setting of
complexity-bounded adversaries. Such a view has remained outside mainstream
cryptography, with interesting but sporadic contributions
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
. On the other
hand, it must be said that a logical account of the notion of
statistical

independence has indeed been proposed very recently by Barthe, Hsu and Liao
[
17
]
,
based on separation logic. In this approach, dubbed
probabilistic separation
logic
(PSL) in the following, separating conjunction is interpreted in the sense of
independence and not in the traditional sense of dynamic data structures.
Probabilistic separation logic is by its nature information-theoretic,
therefore not able to take into account computational notions of independence.
Nonetheless, not only Shannon’s proof, but also much more complex ones, become very
simple and straightforward, being based on some basic principles regarding
probabilistic independence.

It is therefore natural to ask whether and to what extent the approach of

[
17
]

can be adapted to
computational
notions of
independence. This is exactly the aim of this paper.

1.1
Contributions and Outline

The first thing we do, after briefly motivating our work through a couple of
examples, is to introduce in
Section
3
a minimal imperative programming language where all
programs can be evaluated in polytime
by construction
. The language consists only
of assignments, sequential composition, and conditional constructs.
Notably, randomization is captured through function symbols. We take this approach first of all
because it allows the treatment of some interesting examples, and secondly
because the possibility of capturing programs with loops turns out to be by no
means trivial especially from the point of view of managing the adversary’s advantage.

We then proceed, in Section
4
, to a study of how Barthe et
al.’s probabilistic separation logic can be adapted so as to capture
computational, rather than information-theoretic, independence.
The logic stays the same syntactically, while the underlying semantics has to
be modified in a crucial way. More specifically, the partial Kripke resource
monoid from
[
18
]
has to be modified in its order-theoretic part, allowing
computationally indistinguishable distributions to be compared, and thus
implicitly accounting for an approximate notion of independence. These
apparently innocuous modifications have very deep consequences for the
interpretation of separating conjunction. We exclude from formulas both forms of implication,
whose presence would complicate considerably the underlying metatheory, and which are not
particularly useful in proofs. Remarkably, our novel semantic interpretation naturally induces atomic formulas capturing computationally independent pairs of expressions and pseudorandom expressions, this way dealing with fundamental concepts in cryptography in a simple and natural way.

The next section, i.e. Section
5
, instead, concerns the use of our
separation logic as a program logic. More specifically, we study how to
define Hoare triples for the language introduced in Section
3
, and
how to define sound inference rules for such triples. Here, what is surprising is that the rules are not too different from those presented in
[
17
]
. In other words, computational notions can be treated similarly to the corresponding information-theoretic notions. We apply the introduced formal system to a couple of simple examples of cryptographic proofs, namely the security of the encryption scheme induced by a pseudorandom generator, and the proof that suitably composing pseudorandom generators preserve pseudorandomness, at the same time allowing for an increase in the expansion factor.

Finally, Section
7
is concerned with motivating computational independence, and with studying its role in cryptographic proofs. As mentioned above, such an approach was already proposed by Yao
[
3
]
in some of the earliest work on encryption security, although his work used the language of computational entropy. An explicit notion of computational independence was introduced and studied in
[
15
]
. We formulate a notion of computational independence which is in some ways more closely related to the computational semantics of the separating conjunction, and prove that it is equivalent to that of
[
15
]
. Finally, we solve an open problem posed in that paper, namely giving a characterization of semantic security for private-key encryption against
uniform
adversaries
[
19
]
. Overall, the results of this section further support the naturalness of computational independence in a cryptographic setting as well as indicating its utility as an alternate approach to characterizing cryptographic security. This also argues for the naturalness and utility of cryptographic separation logic as a formal counterpart to this computational approach.

2
A Cryptographic Viewpoint on (Probabilistic) Separation Logic

In this section, we give, without going into technical details the
introduction of which is deferred to the next section, a simple
account of separation logic, the probabilistic interpretation of
the latter proposed by Barthe et al., and the possibility of employing
it to derive proofs in the so-called computational model.

Consider the following simple probabilistic program, call it
\mathtt{OTP}
:

\displaystyle k\leftarrow\mathit{rnd}();

\displaystyle c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,k)

This program assigns to the variable
k
, a binary
string of length
n
, one of the
2^{n}
possible values it can take, each with
the same probability. It then uses this value, seen as a key, to construct a
ciphertext
c
starting from a message
m
through the exclusive or operation,
thus implementing the well-known Vernam’s one-time pad, an encryption scheme that is established as
secure in the perfect sense
[
20
]
. This last property can be expressed in various
ways, e.g. by prescribing that the random variables
\mathcal{C}
and

\mathcal{M}
which model the values of
c
and
m
after program execution,
respectively, are
independent
.

Would it be possible to (re)prove the security of such an encryption scheme using
Hoare’s logic? As already mentioned, Barthe et al. gave a positive answer to this question a few years
ago, based on separation logic
[
17
]
. In their framework, one can derive the
following triple

\left\{\mathbf{D}(m)\right\}\;\mathtt{OTP}\;\left\{\mathbf{D}(m)*\mathbf{U}(c)\right\}

This tells us that, whenever messages are distributed according to
any

distribution (this is captured by the precondition
\mathbf{D}(m)
), the ciphertexts
will be uniformly distributed,
\mathbf{U}(c)
, and moreover (and most importantly!)
their distribution will be statistically independent from that on
messages. This latter condition is captured by the binary logical operator

*
, the so-called
separating conjunction
.

The separating conjunction is a connective of
separation
logic

[
21
]
, which is often interpreted in such a
way as to enable reasoning about pointer-manipulating programs: the elements
of the domain are
heaps
representing the state of memory and
the truth of a formula
\phi*\psi
in a store
s
indicates that

s
may be split into two stores, the first satisfying
\phi
and the
second satisfying
\psi
. In Barthe et al.’s work, instead, there is no heap,
each variable contains a piece of data, and formulas are interpreted
over
distributions
of states. In that setting, it is natural for
separating conjunction to model
independence
: a distribution
s
satisfying a formula

\phi*\psi
captures the fact that
s
can be written as the
tensor
product
of two distributions, the first satisfying
\phi
and the
second satisfying
\psi
. In the program above, then,
\mathbf{D}(m)*\mathbf{U}(c)

correctly captures the desired property: the distributions of states over

k,m,c
one obtains after executing
\mathtt{OTP}
can be written as the tensor product
of two distributions, the value of
c
being uniformly
distributed in the second distribution. As such, then, separation logic
correctly captures the perfect security of the OTP.

The question that arises naturally, at this point, is whether this way of
reasoning is also compatible with the computational model, whereas, as
is well known, adversaries are complexity bounded
and their probability of success can be strictly positive, although negligible.
Is it possible, for example, to prove something similar to the aforementioned
independence result for the following simple variation of Vernam’s cipher?

\displaystyle r\leftarrow\mathit{rnd}();

\displaystyle c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(r))

Here, we first pass a random seed
r
to a pseudorandom generator
g
, stretching it
into a stream of bits that, although not random, looks so to any PPT adversary.
Then, we proceed as in Vernam’s cipher. In this way, we are able to use a key of length

n
to encrypt messages of greater length.

The first thing to observe is that it is simply
not
true that for any distribution on messages, the
distributions on messages and ciphertexts are statistically independent.
In particular, far a distribution on messages having full support, since there are fewer keys than messages, we can find a ciphertext
c
which has nonzero probability and a message
m
such that the joint probability of
c
and
m
is zero. This means independence does not hold.

As observed by Yao
[
3
]
, and later studied by Fay
[
15
]
,
however, the aforementioned random variables are independent in a

computational
sense, i.e. their joint distribution, even if impossible to be written as the tensor
product of two distributions, looks so, once again, to the eyes of efficient
adversaries.

This work can be seen as the first attempt to go
in the direction
traced by
Yao and Fay
starting from
Barthe et al.’s PSL. In particular, the latter
will be adapted to the fact that programs and adversaries are
complexity-bounded and that the notion of randomness
is computational,
i.e. equivalent to
pseudorandomness
. Similarly, for
separating conjunction and independence: everything holds in an approximate
sense, the approximation being invisible to the eyes of efficient adversaries
having nonnegligible probability of success.

While this adaptation may apparently seem straightforward, it brings with it a series
of complications. First of all, the class of programs to which
this reasoning can be applied must be drastically restricted, dealing with
conditionals and sequential compositions, but without loops. The very notion of
formula must be narrowed, getting rid of logical implication and restricting
attention to conjunctive formulas. Furthermore, in order to make the splitting of the program unique, we will require formulas to be labeled with type environments. These changes are not motivated by semantic reasons: in fact, BI
can
be given a complexity-conscious semantics in the form of a partial resource monoid, therefore following a well-trodden path. The point is that in BI and its standard semantics proving the soundness of some crucial rules, and in particular the so-called frame rule, turns out to be very difficult compared to doing so for PSL. It was therefore natural to move towards a narrower definitional framework. That being said, simple examples like the one
above can be shown to have the desired independence properties.

A question naturally arises at this point: is computational independence related in any way to mainstream cryptographic notions? Fortunately, the answer is positive: security
of symmetric-key ciphertext can be faithfully captured this way,
making our program logic useful.
This represents the ideal way to complete the picture, and Section
7

is devoted to proving this result.

3
Programs, and their Semantics

In this section, we define programs and their interpretation
as transformers of
efficiently samplable
families
of distributions
[
19
,
20
]
. While doing this,
we also introduce other standard concepts such as sized types and
with the aim of describing their interplay and their notation.

We start by introducing basic notions from probability theory in

Section

3.1.1
. In
Section

3.1.2
,
we define types, environments and stores. Later, in
Section

3.2
and
in
Section

3.3
we define the language of programs together with
its semantics. In
Section

3.5.1
, we show that the semantics of our programs
— when interpreted on ordinary distributions — is equivalent to that of
Kozen
[
23
]
.
In section
Section

3.4
, we introduce some basics concepts of computational
cryprography including
computational indistinguishability
. Finally,

Section

3.5
is devoted to showing cryptographical properties of
our programs.

3.1
Preliminaries

3.1.1
Probabilities and Distributions

In the following, we restrict our analysis to
probability distributions with finite support.
More precisely, each such object is a function

\mathcal{D}:X\to\mathbb{R}_{[0,1]}
that satisfies the following properties:

•

Its support

{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{D})\mathrel{:=}\{x\in X\mid\mathcal{D}(x)>0\}

is finite.

•

\sum_{x\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{D})}\mathcal{D}(x)=1
.

We write
\mathbb{D}(X)
for the set of all distributions over
X
.

The tensor product of two distributions

\mathcal{D}\in\mathbb{D}(X),\mathcal{E}\in\mathbb{D}(Y)
is another distribution, this
time over (
X\times Y
) that samples independently from
\mathcal{D}
and

\mathcal{E}
, i.e.,

(\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{E})\mathrel{:=}(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{D}(x)\cdot\mathcal{E}(y)
.

Probability distributions with finite support form a monad
[
22
]
, where
the unit function
{\text{unit}_{X}(\cdot)}:X\to\mathbb{D}(X)
associates to
its argument
x\in X
the Dirac distribution centered on
x
and the
multiplication
\text{bind}_{X}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{D}(X)\times(X\to\mathbb{D}(Z))\to\mathbb{D}(Z)
sequences
\mathcal{D}\in\mathbb{D}(X)
with a continuation

f:X\to\mathbb{D}(Z)
.

This property induces the existence of the Kleisli extension
of each function
f:X\to\mathbb{D}(Y)
: this is a function

f^{\dagger}:\mathbb{D}(X)\to\mathbb{D}(Y)
such that

f^{\dagger}(\mathcal{D})=\text{bind}_{X}(\mathcal{D},f)
.
Given a distribution
\mathcal{D}\in\mathbb{D}(X)
and a function
f:X\to Y
, we write

f(\mathcal{D})
to denote
y\mapsto\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}\mathcal{D}(x)
.

3.1.2
Types

In this section, we define types and environments, together with
their interpretation as
families
of distributions.
Programs and stores are indeed typed to enforce size bounds to variables, depending
on the value of the so-called
security parameter
.
For instance, if the type associated to the variable
r
by
the environment
\Delta
is
\mathsf{Str}[2n]
,
the size of the binary string associated to

\Delta
by a store of type
r
must be

exactly

2n
, i.e.
two times the length of the security parameter.
By allowing only types whose size is polynomial in
the size of the security parameter, we ensure that
the evaluation of
well-typed
programs on
well-typed
stores
is polytime with respect to the size of the security parameter.

Univariate positive polynomials
are indicated by metavariables
like
p
and
q
; we write
p(n)
for the natural number
obtained by evaluating the polynomial
p
on the argument
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Types

are defined from the following grammar:

\tau::=\mathsf{Str}[p]\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\mathsf{Bool}.

where
p
is a univariate polynomial. Let
\mathsf{T}
be the set of all types.
Given
\tau
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\llbracket\mathsf{Str}[p]\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}=\mathbb{B}^{p(n)}
,

\llbracket\mathsf{Bool}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}=\mathbb{B}
,
and
\mathbb{B}=\{0,1\}
is the set of binary digits.

The set of program variables is
\mathcal{V}
, and it is
ranged over by metavariables like
r,s
.
An
environment

\Delta:\mathcal{V}\rightharpoonup\mathsf{T}

is a partial function with finite domain indicated as
\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
.
We write
\varepsilon
for the empty environment, and we
also adopt standard abbreviations for environments in the form
of lists of assignments of types to variables, e.g.

r_{1}:\tau_{1},\ldots,r_{k}:\tau_{k}
.
We introduce a partial order relation between
environments:
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta
holds
whenever
\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)

and for every
r\in\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)

we have
\Xi(r)=\Delta(r)
.
The (disjoint) union of two environments
\Delta,\Xi
is
indicated as
\Delta\Join\Xi
and is
defined
only
if

\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)\cap\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)=\emptyset
;
when this happens, it is defined in the standard way.

It is now time to turn to the interpretation of types, which will be built
around the notion of an efficiently samplable distribution family.
This is a standard notion in complexity-based cryptography (see Subsection
3.4
) and is captured by the following definition:

Definition 1
.

A family of distributions
\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

is
efficiently samplable
when
there is
one
probabilistic polytime algorithm
that outputs samples distributed according to
\mathcal{D}_{n}
on input

1^{n}
. In this case, we write
\mathsf{ES}({\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}})
.

\hfill\qed

The semantics of a type
\tau
,
which we call
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
is the class
of (efficiently samplable) families

\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\mathcal{D}_{n}\in\mathbb{D}(\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}})
for
every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Formally:

\llbracket\tau\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mid\mathsf{ES}({\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}})\land\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\mathcal{D}_{n}\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}\}.

In the following, we will often abbreviate
\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

as just
\mathcal{D}
.

We can extend the semantics above from types to environments.
An environment
\Delta
is interpreted as a set of
distribution ensembles

in the following way:

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\{\{\mathcal{R}_{n}\}_{n}\mid\mathsf{ES}({\mathcal{R}})\land\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\mathcal{R}_{n}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}\}

where
\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}=\mathbb{D}(\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}})
, and

\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
is the set of
well-typed maps from variables to values, i.e.,

\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\mathrel{:=}\{m\in\left(\mathbb{B}^{*}\right)^{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}\mid\forall r\in{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}.m(r)\in\llbracket\Delta(r)\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\}.

Example 1
.

If
\Delta=r:\mathsf{Str}[n],s:\mathsf{Str}[2n]
,
then the semantics of
\Delta
is the set of all distribution ensembles

\{\mathcal{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that, for each
n
,

\mathcal{R}_{n}
is a distribution in
\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}
.
This means that every member of this family
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is a distribution of
functions
m:\{r,s\}\to{\mathbb{B}^{*}}
such that

m(r)
is a binary string of length
n
and

m(s)
is a binary string of length
2n
.
We also require
\{\mathcal{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to be

effectively samplable
, so there must be a polytime
probabilistic algorithm that, on input
1^{n}
, produces
samples according to
\mathcal{R}_{n}
. ∎

In our logic, to state that some expression of type
\tau

is pseudorandom, we compare it against the family of uniform distributions
of strings of that type, and we impose that the advantage of any
polytime distinguisher for the two distributions is a negligible function (
Definition

6
).

In turn, the (effectively samplable) family of
uniform distributions strings of a given type
\tau
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Bool})_{n}(b)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\text{with }b\in\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)])_{n}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}{\frac{1}{2^{p(n)}}}

\displaystyle\text{with }x\in\mathbb{B}^{p(n)}.

3.1.3
Stores

Intuitively, stores can be defined just as elements of

\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
. But, since we want to reason about
stores with
different
domains, we also make the
domain
\Delta
explicit in the notation by
defining
stores
as pairs in the form
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}

where
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
.
We write
\mathbf{0}
for the store

{(\{\{\varepsilon^{1}\}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}})^{\varepsilon}}

where
\{\varepsilon^{1}\}
is the distribution assigning probability
1
to

\varepsilon
, namely the only element of
\llbracket\varepsilon\rrbracket_{n}
. The
set

\mathcal{S}
stands for the set of all stores.
In the following, we use metavariables like

s,r,t
for stores.

The partial order
\sqsubseteq

can be lifted to distribution
ensembles as follows:
we say that a probabilistic store

s={(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}
extends a
store
r={(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}
,
written
r\sqsubseteq s
, if and only if
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta
and

\mathcal{Q}
can be obtained by computing the marginal distribution of

\mathcal{R}
on the domain of
\Xi
, itself often indicated
as
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi}
.
In turn, the definition of projection on distribution
ensembles is lifted to stores by setting

\left({(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\right)_{\Delta\to\Xi}={(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})^{\Xi}}
.

Definition 2

(Projection)
.

Given a store
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}
and
\Xi
such that
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta
, we define the projection
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)_{\Delta\to\Xi}\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
, as follows:

\left(\mathcal{R}\right)_{\Delta\to\Xi}\mathrel{:=}\left\{\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})})\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

where for every set of random variables
\mathsf{S}
,

m_{\mathsf{S}}
is the function obtained
by restricting the domain of

m
to
\mathsf{S}
.

The relation
\sqsubseteq

is widely employed
in Section
4
,
because it allows our logic to describe

local
properties, i.e. properties that are valid in

portions of stores
.

We can define the tensor product of two
stores
as follows:

Definition 3
.

The pointwise tensor product of
two distribution ensembles
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}

and
{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}
such that
\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)\cap\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)=\emptyset

is defined as a distribution ensemble
{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\mathrel{\otimes}{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}
in

\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\rrbracket
, as follows:

({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\mathrel{\otimes}{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}})_{n}(m)\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{R}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}).

We write
s\otimes r\downarrow
when the tensor product of two stores
is defined.
∎

3.2
Expressions

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash r:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}{m(r)}

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash f(d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}):\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathit{DetAlgoOf}(f)(1^{n})(\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d_{1}:\tau_{1}\rrparenthesis_{n}(m),\ldots,\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d_{k}:\tau_{k}\rrparenthesis_{n}(m))

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}{\text{unit}(\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m))}

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\vdash f(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}):\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathit{AlgoOf}(f){}(1^{n})^{\dagger}({\llbracket\Delta\vdash e_{1}:\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)\mathrel{\otimes}\ldots\mathrel{\otimes}\llbracket\Delta\vdash e_{k}:\tau_{k}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)})

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{skip}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}\mapsto\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}\mapsto\text{bind}(\mathcal{D},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}\mapsto\llbracket{\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}}\rrbracket_{n}\left(\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\right)(\mathcal{D})\right)

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{D}\mapsto{\text{bind}\left(\mathcal{D},m\mapsto\begin{cases}\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}\right)}

Figure 1:
Semantics of Expressions and Programs

Before introducing programs,
we describe the

expressions
that occur in them. The set
\mathcal{F}
of
all
function symbols
is assumed to be given, and
partitioned into two subsets
\mathcal{DF}
and
\mathcal{RF}
, of
deterministic and randomized function symbols, respectively.
The function
\mathit{TypeOf}(\cdot)
provides the type of every function symbol
in
\mathcal{F}
, i.e,

\mathit{TypeOf}(f)=\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\rightarrow\sigma.

For every deterministic function symbol
f\in\mathcal{DF}
,
such that

\mathit{TypeOf}(f)=\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\rightarrow\sigma
,
we assume the existence of a deterministic algorithm
\mathit{DetAlgoOf}(f)

that, on input
(1^{n},s)
where
s\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\times\ldots\times\llbracket\tau_{k}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
,
returns a value
t\in\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
. In order
not to violate the time constraint, we assume
that
\mathit{DetAlgoOf}(f)
is polytime in the size of its first argument
for every
f\in\mathcal{DF}
.
Similarly, for every
f\in\mathcal{RF}
, we
assume the existence
of an efficient
randomized
algorithm
\mathit{AlgoOf}(f)

that, on input
(1^{n},s)
where

s\in\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\times\ldots\times\llbracket\tau_{k}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
, returns a
distribution in
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{n}
.

The following primitive operations, with self-explanatory behavior, will be useful below:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathit{rnd}&:\varepsilon\rightarrow\mathsf{Str}[n]&\mathit{tail}_{p+1}&:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)+1]\rightarrow\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]\\
\mathit{head}_{p}&:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)+1]\rightarrow\mathsf{Bool}&\mathit{xor}&:\mathsf{Str}[n]\times\mathsf{Str}[n]\rightarrow\mathsf{Str}[n]\\
\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathit{concat}_{p,q}:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]\times\mathsf{Str}[q(n)]\rightarrow\mathsf{Str}[p(n)+q(n)]

\displaystyle\mathit{setzero}_{p}:\varepsilon\rightarrow\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]

The only randomized primitive operation among these is
\mathit{rnd}
, which simply returns

n
independently and uniformly distributed random bits. Noticed that these functions
may be indexed by some polynomials that describe the size of their arguments.
We omit these annotations, when the sizes of the arguments are evident from the context.

Deterministic and random expressions
are defined through the following grammars:

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle::=r\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;g(d_{1},\ldots,d_{k})

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle::=d\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;f(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k})

where
r\in\mathcal{V},g\in\mathcal{DF},f\in\mathcal{RF}
.
A simple type system with
judgments in the form

\Delta\vdash e:\tau
can be given as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta\vdash r:\tau\Delta(r)=\tau\quad\Delta\vdash f(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}):\sigma\mathit{TypeOf}(f)=\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\rightarrow\sigma\left\{\Delta\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq k}

We write
\mathsf{FV}(e)
for the set of free variables of
e
.
One can define the semantics of typed expressions in a standard way, as
formalized in the following definition.

Definition 4

(Semantics of Expressions)
.

The semantics of a
deterministic expression

d

is a family of functions
\{\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}:\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\to\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
.
The
pre-semantics
of a general expression
\Delta\vdash e:\tau
, is a family
\left\{\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}:{\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}}\to{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}}
, see Figure
1
.
The semantics of a randomized expression
e
, indicated as

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket
, is the family of functions

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}:\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}\to{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}}
, indexed by
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}
is the Kleisli extension of

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
.
∎

3.3
Programs

We work within a probabilistic imperative language without iteration.
(We discuss in Section
8
below why dealing with loops is nontrivial.)

Programs
are defined by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\mathtt{P},\mathtt{R}::=\;\mathtt{skip}\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;r\leftarrow e\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\mathtt{P};\mathtt{P}\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{P}

A type system with judgments in the form
\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}

for the just defined programs can be
given in a standard way.
Programs can be endowed with a denotational semantics.

Definition 5
.

Whenever
\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}
, the semantics of
\mathtt{P}
is
the family
\left\{\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
where for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}:\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}\to\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}

as in Figure
1
.
This function induces an endofunction on
\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
which we indicate as
\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket
and which is defined as

\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right)_{n}\mathrel{:=}\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
.
Very often,
\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}
(resp.
\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket
)
will be abbreviated just as
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}
(resp.
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket
).
∎

The interpretations of
\mathtt{skip}
and of the composition
\mathtt{P}\mathtt{R};

are trivial. The interpretation of assignments is similar to that of
sampling statements in
[
17
]
; it composes the initial distribution
with the continuation that associates every
m
in the support of
the initial distribution
with the store that is obtained by evaluating
e
on
m

and updating the value of
r
in
m
accordingly.
Finally, the interpretation of the conditional constructs
composes the distribution with the function that,
given an element of its support, executes
\mathtt{P}
on
it if this program satisfies the guard,
and executes
\mathtt{R}
otherwise.
Overall, the definition of our semantics is close to that of Kozen
[
23
]
,
but that the semantics of the conditional construct
is not given in terms of conditional distributions.
This is due to the need of having an endofunction on

efficiently samplable
distribution ensembles
as the semantics of our language.

Specifically, we cannot use conditioned distributions
because, given a polytime sampler for the initial distribution
ensemble
\mathcal{R}
, we may not be able to identify a polytime
sampler for the distribution ensemble obtained by conditioning
\mathcal{R}
on
the value of the guard. For instance, although a construction
based on rejection sampling would produce the desired output distribution,
it would not be
polytime
in general.

In
Section

3.5.1
below, we show that the two definitions are
equivalent when evaluated
on distributions
.

Notice that, by construction, for every program
\mathtt{P}
,

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket
can be seen as a family of circuits,
and for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the circuit
\llbracket{\mathtt{P}}\rrbracket_{n}

has polynomial size in
n
.

3.4
Notions from Computational Cryptography

One of our main goals is to provide a formal framework for reasoning about definitions and constructions in complexity-based cryptography, in this work we follow a standard approach (e.g., as presented in
[
20
]
or
[
19
]
). A fundamental characteristic of this setting is that all parties, both honest and adversarial, are modeled as efficient (randomized) agents, in particular as
probabilistic polynomial time
(PPT) algorithms. This framework underlies the notion of samplablability given in Definition
1
, as such distributions are exactly those sampled by some PPT algorithm.

While it is well-known that under reasonable restrictions (e.g., on the length of keys) it is impossible to provide absolute guarantees against adversarial success, definitions of security in the computational setting require
negligible
adversarial advantage, where negligibilty is measured as a function of a
security parameter

n\in\mathbb{N}
. This value measures the size of the secret information used by honest parties (e.g. the length of a secret key). In keeping with the asymptotic complexity bounds on adversaries, this leads to the following:

Definition 6
.

A function
\nu:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is negligible if for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for sufficiently large
n
,
\nu(n)\leq 1/n^{k}
.
∎

A fundamental concept in cryptography is a binary relation on distribution
ensembles called computational indistinguishability, by which we mean that no
probabilistic polynomial time algorithm
can distinguish between the two ensembles with non-negligible
probability of success. Here, we can introduce computational
indistinguishability as a relation on stores:

Definition 7
.

We say that two distribution ensembles
\{\mathcal{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\{\mathcal{Q}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket

(resp. in
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
) are indistinguishable,
and we write
\{\mathcal{R}_{n}\}_{n}\approx\{\mathcal{Q}_{n}\}_{n}
, if and only if
for every probabilistic polynomial time algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
,
there is a negligible function
{\mathit{negl}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left|{\text{Pr}_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},x)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{Q}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},x)=1\right]\right|\leq{\mathit{negl}}(n).

With this definition at hand, we are able to define a central notion in complexity-based cryptography:
pseudorandomness
.
Let
\{\mathcal{U}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
denote the distribution ensemble where
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is the uniform distribution on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

Definition 8
.

A distribution ensemble
\{\mathcal{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is pseudorandom if

\{\mathcal{R}_{n}\}_{n}\approx\{\mathcal{U}_{n}\}_{n}
.
A (deterministic) poly-time function
f:\mathbb{B}^{*}\to\mathbb{B}^{*}
is a pseudorandom generator if there is a function

\ell:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with
\ell(n)>n

such that for
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
f(x)\in\mathbb{B}^{\ell(n)}
and

\{f(\mathcal{U}_{n})\}_{n}\approx\{\mathcal{U}_{\ell(n)}\}_{n}
.
∎

Thus, a distribution ensemble is pseudorandom if no PPT algorithm can distinguish between samples from the distribution and samples from a truly random distribution.

A more detailed discussion of complexity-based cryptography is given in Section
7
.

3.5
Semantic Properties of Programs

This section is devoted to showing some remarkable properties
of our programs’ semantics. We start by showing that
it is equivalent to the one proposed by Kozen
[
23
]
.

In
Sections

3.5.2
and
3.5.3
, we show
some other general properties of the expression of expressions
and programs, notably these include
Lemmas

5
and
6
,
stating that the outcome of the evaluation of expressions
and programs does not depend on those variables that do
not appear in the program, and
Lemmas

10
and
2
, where we show that
the distribution ensemble that is obtained by evaluating the
same program (resp. expression) on two computationally
indistinguishable ensembles are themselves computationally indistinguishable.

3.5.1
Equivalence with Kozen’s Semantics

In this subsection, we are interested in showing that our semantics
is equivalent to that by Kozen
[
23
]
.
A subtle difference within these two semantics is that,
while that by Kozen is defined on
ordinary
distributions, ours is
defined on efficiently samplable distributions. This means that,
with our semantics,
if we take an efficiently samplable distribution,
and we compute the semantics of a program on it, we obtain
another efficiently samplable distribution.
In contrast to our semantics, Kozen’s one is defined
on
ordinary
distributions, so
it does not provide any guarantee on the
existence of such a sampler.
However, the equivalence between these two semantics

seen on regular distributions
has as a consequence that
the loopless fragment of Kozen’s language (and semantics) transforms
efficiently samplable distributions in other
efficiently samplable distributions.
This result is not trivial because, on conditional statements,
Kozen’s semantic of the
\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}

computes the conditioned distributions of
s
on the

r=1
and
r=0
obtaining two states
s_{1}
and
s_{0}
,
Finally, it outputs the convex combination of
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({s_{1}}){}

and
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({s_{0}}){}
according to
\llbracket r\rrbracket({s}){}
.
In particular, as far as we know, it is not known whether
efficiently samplable distributions are closed under conditioning.

The first step we do in order to establish this claim,
is to define the conditioning operation:
given a distribution ensemble
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
we write
{{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=b}}

for the distribution that is obtained by conditioning
\mathcal{R}_{n}

on the event
m(r)=\mathsf{Bool}
. Formally, this distribution is
defined as follows:

{{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=b}}(m)\mathrel{:=}\begin{cases}\frac{{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(m)}{{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(S)}&\text{if }m(r)=b\text{ and }S=\{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})\mid m(r)=b\}\neq\emptyset\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Finally, we define the convex combination of two distributions

\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}\in\mathbb{D}(X)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
as follows:

\left(\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\oplus_{d}}\mathcal{E}\right)(\vec{z})\mathrel{:=}d(1)\cdot\mathcal{D}(x)+d(0)\cdot\mathcal{E}(x).

Definition 9
.

The Kozen-style definition of the semantics of loopless program (
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\mathtt{K}}_{\cdot}
) is defined identically to that of
Figure

1
, except for
\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}
, which is defined as follows:

\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket^{\mathtt{K}}_{n}\mathrel{:=}\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket^{\mathtt{K}}_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})&\text{if }\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})(1)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket^{\mathtt{K}}_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})&\text{if }\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})(1)=0\\
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket^{\mathtt{K}}_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=1}})\mathrel{\oplus_{\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})}}\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket^{\mathtt{K}}_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=0}})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In order to show the equivalence we are aiming to,
we also need to introduce some results on measures:
given two measures
\mu,\nu
, we write
\mu+\nu
as
a shorthand for
x\mapsto\mu(x)+\nu(x)
, and for
every
k\in[0,1]
, we write
k\cdot\mu
for

x\mapsto k\cdot\mu(n,x)
.
By considering the extension of our program’s semantics
to measures, we obtain the following result:

Remark 1
.

Let
\mathtt{P}
be a program, and
n
a natural number. It holds that:

•

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mu+\nu)=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mu)+\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\nu)

•

\forall 0\leq k\leq 1.\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(k\cdot\mu)=k\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mu)

Proof.

By induction on
\mathtt{P}
.
∎

Leveraging this Remark, it is possible to show the
main result of this section, the following lemma:

Lemma 1
.

The semantics of programs, seen as in Definition
5
, if interpreted on
ordinary
distributions,
is equivalent to that by Kozen,
[
23
]
.

Proof.

We go by induction on
\mathtt{P}
. Whenever
\mathtt{P}
is not a conditional,
the conclusion is trivial because the two semantics are identical by
definition. For this reason, we take in exam only the case where the
program is
\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}
. We first assume that the
guard is identically 1 or 0. In those cases ewe are required to verify that:

{\text{bind}\left(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}\right)}

Is equal to
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
or respectively to
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})

we assume without lack of generality that the distribution of the guard is identically 1.
In this case we are asked to show that:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)}))=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}).

A formal proof of this result is in
Lemma

12
below; notice that the proof of that lemma does not rely on this lemma.

Now, we assume that the guard is not identically 1 or 0.
In this case, the claim is:
\forall\Delta.\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\forall\mathcal{R}_{n}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}.\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=1}})\mathrel{\oplus_{\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})}}\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=0}})
.
We start by fixing
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
,
and we observe that, by expanding the definition of the semantics, we obtain that

\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})(n,m)
is equal to:

\displaystyle\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=1}\;\;{\mathcal{R}(n,l)}\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})(m)+\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=0}\;\;{\mathcal{R}(n,l)}\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})(m)

On the other hand, we can expand the definition of

\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=1}})\mathrel{\oplus_{\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})}}\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=0}})\right)(m)

as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=1}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=1}})(m)+\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=0}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=0}})(m)

Now, we can simply show that for every program
\mathtt{P}
and Boolean
b
:

\displaystyle\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})(m)=\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({s_{{}|r=b}}(n))(m)

(
*
)

We start by simplifying the expression on the right. First, we observe that, for Remark
1
,
{{\mathcal{R}_{n}}_{{}|r=b}}(m)

is equal to:

\frac{1}{\sum_{w:w\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land w(r)=b}\mathcal{R}_{n}(w)}\cdot\begin{cases}{\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)}&\text{if }m(r)=b\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Let
k_{b}\in[0,1]
and
\mathcal{T}_{b}
be defined as follows:

k_{b}\mathrel{:=}\frac{1}{\sum_{w:w\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land w(r)=b}\mathcal{R}_{n}(w)}\quad\quad\quad\mathcal{T}_{b}(m)\mathrel{:=}\begin{cases}{\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)}&\text{if }m(r)=b\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

With these definitions, the expression we were reducing can be rewritten as

\ldots=\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(k_{b}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{b})(m)\\

from the observation above and Remark
1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\ldots=\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot k_{b}\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{T}_{b})(m)=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{T}_{b})(m),

from the definition of
k_{b}
.
We call
\mathcal{Q}^{m}
the distribution ensemble defined as follows:

\mathcal{Q}^{m}_{n}(w)\mathrel{:=}\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}_{n}({m})&\text{if }m=w\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Observe that:

\mathcal{T}_{b}(w)=\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\;\;\mathcal{Q}^{l}_{n}(w),

so we can continue the reduction of the term observing that:

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{T}_{b})=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\left(\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\mathcal{Q}^{l}_{n}\right)

With an application of Remark
1
we can state the following equivalence:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{T}_{b})=\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\;\;\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{l}_{n}\right),

finally, we observe that
\mathcal{Q}^{l}_{n}(m)=\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(l)}(m)
so, we have:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{T}_{b})=\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\land l(r)=b}\;\;\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{l}_{n}\right)=\sum_{l:l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}};\land l(r)=b}\;\;\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\left(m\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(l)}(m)\right).

So, in order to show our claim, it suffices to observe that for every
l\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
such that
l(r)=b
, we have:

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\left(m\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(l)}(m)\right)=\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}\left({\text{unit}(l)}\right).

But this is a consequence of
m\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(l)}(m)=m\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{n}(l)\cdot{\text{unit}(l)}(m)
and of
Remark

1
.
∎

3.5.2
Properties of Expressions

We start by showing that if
s\approx r
, then

\llbracket e\rrbracket(s)\approx\llbracket e\rrbracket(r)
(
Lemma

2
)
and that, if
s\sqsubseteq r
then

\llbracket e\rrbracket(s)=\llbracket e\rrbracket(r)
(
Lemma

5
).
These properties in turn, are useful later
to show that the semantic of specific CSL’s formulas
is closed with respect to
\approx\sqsubseteq
(
Lemma

24
).
Leveraging this result, we show that our model also enjoys
a form Restriction Property (
Corollary

1
), and
that some standard axiom schema for atomic formulas hold,
Lemma

25
.

Lemma 2

(Indistinguishability of Expressions’ Semantic)
.

For every expression
e
, and every pair of stores
{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}
such that
\Delta\vdash e:\tau,\Xi\vdash e:\tau
it holds that
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\approx\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{Q})
.

Proof.

Assume the claim not to hold, this means that there is an adversary
{\mathcal{A}}

which is capable to distinguish
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
and
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})

with non-negligible advantage.
This adversary can be used to define an adversary
{\mathcal{B}}

with the same advantage on
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{Q}
.
On input
(1^{n},m)
, it samples from
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)
and then
executes
{\mathcal{A}}
on this sample.
The advantage of
{\mathcal{B}}
on
\Delta,\Xi
is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}

on
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R})
and
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{Q})
.
Notice that since
e
is polytime computable,
{\mathcal{B}}
is also
polytime.
∎

Then, we show that evaluation expression
e
such that

\Delta\vdash e:\tau
in a state
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}

yields the same result of evaluating it in a state

({(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}})_{\Delta\to\Xi}
if
g\vdash e:\tau
.

Lemma 3
.

For every deterministic expression
d
each pair of environments
\Delta\sqsubseteq\Xi
such that
\Delta\vdash d:\tau
and
\Xi\vdash d:\tau
, every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every tuple
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket_{n})
, it holds that
\llparenthesis\Xi\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the syntax of
d

•

If
d
is
r
, we observe that
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})

\displaystyle=(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})(r)=m(r)

because
r
in
\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
from the assumption
\Delta\vdash e:\tau
.

•

If
d
is a complex expression, the claim is a consequence of the IHs.

∎

Lemma 4
.

For every expression
e
each pair of environments
\Delta\sqsubseteq\Xi
such that
\Delta\vdash e:\tau
and
\Xi\vdash e:\tau
, and every tuple
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket_{n})
, it holds that
\llbracket\Xi\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket^{\mathtt{d}}(m)=\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket^{\mathtt{d}}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the syntax of
e

•

If
e
is a deterministic expression, we observe that
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\llbracket\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})

\displaystyle={\text{unit}(\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}))}

\displaystyle={\text{unit}(\llparenthesis\Xi\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m))}.

•

If
e
is a complex expression, the claim is a consequence of the IHs.

∎

Lemma 5

(Expressions’ Semantic on projection)
.

For every expression
e
such that
\Delta\vdash e:\tau
and
\Xi\vdash e:\tau
, and every store
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})=\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

We start by expanding the definition of
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})
for some
t\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}
:

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=\text{bind}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}),\vec{y}\mapsto\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(\vec{y}))

\displaystyle=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}({\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})},\vec{y}\mapsto\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(\vec{y})))

\displaystyle=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto{\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}({m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}})})

It holds that
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}({m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}})=\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)
for Lemma
4
, so we can conclude the proof.
∎

We conclude this section by showing a technical result that is employed in the proof of soundness of the rules

Remark 2
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, deterministic expression
d
, every
\Delta
such that
\Delta\vdash d:\tau
, and every
r\in\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
such that
r\notin\mathsf{FV}(d)
, every
m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
, and every
t\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
, we have that
\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m[r\mapsto t])
.

Proof.

We start by fixing
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The proof is by induction on
d
. If it is a variable, we are sure that it must be different from
r
, so the conclusion is trivial. In the inductive case, the conclusion is a direct consequence of the IHs on the sub-expressions.
∎

3.5.3
Semantic Properties of Programs

In this section, we show some remarkable properties of our programs’ semantics. We start with
Lemma

6
, where we show that the semantic of a program depends only on those variables that are used to type that program.
In
Lemmas

7
and
8
, we show the soundness of the function
\mathsf{MV}(\cdot)
: in particular, we show that the program variables that do not belong to
\mathsf{MV}(\cdot)
are not affected by the evaluation of
\mathtt{P}
.
Another result with the same spirit is
Lemma

9
, where we show that if a program
\mathtt{P}
is evaluated on the tensor product of two distribution ensembles
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{Q}
, and the domain on one of them contains only variables that
\mathtt{P}
does not use, then in the resulting distribution ensemble, these variables are still independent from the others.

Lemma

10
states that by evaluating the same program on two computationally indistinguishable stores, we obtain two computationally indistinguishable outputs. Crucially, it is possible to show this result because our programs are polytime.

In
Lemma

11
, we show that after an assignment of an expression to a variable
r
, such that
r\notin\mathsf{FV}(e)
the values obtained by evaluating
r
and
e
are the same.
Lemmas

12
,
13
and
14
are devoted to show a similar claim, but for deterministic expressions. These results and
Lemma

15
are employed in the proof of soundness of the rules for assignments in
Section

5
.

Lemma 6
.

For every program
\mathtt{P}
, and every store
{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}
such that
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta
,
\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}
, it holds that
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\to\Xi}
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on the program.

•

If
\mathtt{P}=\mathtt{skip}
, the conclusion is trivial.

•

If
\mathtt{P}=r\leftarrow e
, observe that:

\displaystyle(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}))_{\Delta\to\Xi}

\displaystyle=\text{bind}(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}),m\mapsto m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}).

By expanding the definition of the semantics and applying some monadic laws, it is possible to deduce that it is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}({m[r\mapsto t]}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})})),

on the other hand, with similar expansions, we obtain that
(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi}))
is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Xi\vdash e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}),t\mapsto{\text{unit}({m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}[r\mapsto t]})})),

and with an application of
Lemma

4
, we observe that, this is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}({m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}[r\mapsto t]})})).

By observing
\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}
, we obtain that
r\in\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)
, which is enough to state the equivalence.

•

The case of composition is a trivial consequence of the IH.

•

If
\mathtt{P}
is a conditional we are required to show that:

\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\to\Xi}=\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})

The term on the right can be reduced as follows:

\text{bind}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto h(m)),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})})

where

h(m)=\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}

which is equal to:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(h(m),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}))

and, by definition of projection, to:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto h(m)_{\Delta\to\Xi}).

As we did of assignments, we can conclude the
proof by assessing that the term above is equal to:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto h(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}))

(
*
)

Observe that:

h(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})=\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)}_{\Delta\to\Xi})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({{\text{unit}(m)}_{\Delta\to\Xi}})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}

We continue by proceed applying the IHs, to conclude that (
*
) is equal to:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto h(m)_{\Delta\to\Xi}).

∎

Lemma 7
.

\forall\Delta.\forall\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta.\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\Rightarrow r\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)\Rightarrow\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}}_{\Delta\to\Xi}=({\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})_{\Delta\to\Xi}.

Proof.

Observe that:

\displaystyle(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}))_{\Delta\to\Xi}

\displaystyle=\text{bind}(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}),m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}).

By expanding the definition of the semantics and applying standard monads’ properties, it is possible to deduce that it is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}(n),m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket^{\mathtt{d}}(n,m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}({{m[r\mapsto t]}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}})})),

which, in turn, is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}(n),m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket^{\mathtt{d}}(n,m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}({m}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}))

for the assumption on
r
.
It is easy to verify that, since
{t\mapsto{\text{unit}({m}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}}
is a constant function, the expression above is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}(n),m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}).

∎

Lemma 8

(Modified Variables and Projection)
.

It holds that:

\forall\mathtt{P},\Delta.\forall\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta.\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\Rightarrow\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)\cap\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{P})=\emptyset\Rightarrow\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{.}{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}}_{\Delta\to\Xi}={\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})}_{\Delta\to\Xi}.

Proof.

This proof is by induction on
\mathtt{P}
.

•

If
\mathtt{P}
is
\mathtt{skip}
, then the premise coincides with the conclusion.

•

If
\mathtt{P}
is
\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}
, then the claim comes from the application of the IH on
\mathtt{P}
and of that on
\mathtt{R}
.

•

If the program is
\mathtt{if}\;e\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}
, we start by expanding
\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\to\Xi}
as follows:

\text{bind}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto h(m)),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})})

where

h(m)=\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}

which is equal to:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(h(m),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}))

Now, it is possible to observe that
\text{bind}(h(m),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})})
is equal to:

\begin{cases}\text{bind}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)}),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\text{bind}(\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)}),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})})&\text{if }m(r)=0,\end{cases}

which in turn is equal to

\begin{cases}{\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})}_{\Delta\to\Xi}&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
{\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})}_{\Delta\to\Xi}&\text{if }m(r)=0.\end{cases}

The conclusion follows from the IHs.

•

\mathtt{P}
is
r\leftarrow e
, the claim is a consequence of Lemma
7
.

∎

Lemma 9
.

For every program
\mathtt{P}
, and every pair of stores
s={{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}
,
r={{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}
such that
\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}
and
s\otimes r\downarrow
, it holds that
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(s\otimes r)=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(s)\otimes r
.

Proof.

We will show that

\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}((\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})_{n})=\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q}\right)_{n}.

The proof goes by induction on
\mathtt{P}
.

•

The case where
\mathtt{P}=\mathtt{skip}
is trivial.

•

When
\mathtt{P}=r\leftarrow e
, we observe that:

\displaystyle\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}((\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})_{n})

\displaystyle=\text{bind}((\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])}))

\displaystyle=\text{bind}((\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])}))

Observe that
{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])}
is equal to
l\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}[r\mapsto t])}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}){\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})
, so we can expand the expressions and obtain:

l\mapsto\;\;\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\rrbracket_{n})}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})\cdot\\
\sum_{t\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}}\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})(t)\cdot{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}[r\mapsto t])}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})\cdot\\
{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})

By using the distributive property of multiplication, it is possible to observe that this is equal to:

\displaystyle l\mapsto\;\;\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})({l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}})\cdot\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket_{n})}\;\;\mathcal{Q}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(m)}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}),

which, in turn, is equal to:

l\mapsto\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})({l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}})\cdot\text{bind}(\mathcal{Q}_{n},{\text{unit}(\cdot)})(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})=\\
l\mapsto\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})({l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}})\cdot{\mathcal{Q}_{n}}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}).

This, in turn, is equal to:

\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\mathrel{\otimes}{\mathcal{Q}}\right)_{n}.

•

When the program is
\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}
, the claim is a direct consequence of the IHs.

•

When the program is
\mathtt{if}\;e\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}
,
\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;e\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}((\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})_{n})
is equivalent to:

l\mapsto\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}{\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\rrbracket_{n}}}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})\cdot h(m)(l)

where

h(m)=\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}

Observe that
h(m)=h^{\prime}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)},m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})
, where

h^{\prime}(l_{1},l_{2})=\ \begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l_{1})}\mathrel{\otimes}{\text{unit}(l_{2})})&\text{if }l_{1}(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l_{1})}\mathrel{\otimes}{\text{unit}(l_{2})})&\text{if }l_{1}(r)=0\\
\end{cases}

By using the IH, we conclude that this is equal to:

h^{\prime}(l_{1},l_{2})=\ \begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l_{1})})\mathrel{\otimes}{\text{unit}(l_{2})}&\text{if }l_{1}(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l_{1})})\mathrel{\otimes}{\text{unit}(l_{2})}&\text{if }l_{1}(r)=0\\
\end{cases}

So we can rewrite the expression we wrote at the beginning as follows:

l\mapsto\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}{\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}}}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot h(m)(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})\cdot\\
\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}{\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket_{n}}}\;\;\mathcal{Q}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(m)}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}),

which is:

l\mapsto\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}{\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}}}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot h(m)(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{n}(m)(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)}).

This last term is equal, by definition, to:

\left(\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;e\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q}\right)_{n}.

The conclusion comes by observing that

\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(s\otimes r)={(\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q}))^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}
and that
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(s)\otimes r={(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}
. So, in particular:

\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})=\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q}

is a consequence of (C).
∎

Lemma 10

(Indistinguishability of Programs’ Semantic)
.

For every program
\mathtt{P}
, and every pair of stores
{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}
such that
\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P},\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}
, it holds that
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\approx\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{Q})
.

Proof.

Observe that the semantics of programs we gave can be defined in terms of the Kleisli extension of a distribution ensemble with poly-time samplable continuations. In particular, this can be shown by induction on the semantics of programs observing that:

•

the claim is trivial for
\mathtt{skip}

•

it is a consequence of the poly-time samplability property of the expressions’ semantics in the case of assignments, and on the observation that updating a sample can be done in polynomial time

•

it is a direct consequence of the IH in the case of the composition,

•

in the case of the conditional statement, it is a consequence of the IHs, and of the observation that testing a variable on that sample can be done in polynomial time

This means that for every program
\mathtt{P}
, there is a polynomial transformation on samples
t_{\mathtt{P}}
such that:

\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})={\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},t_{\mathtt{P}})}

Assume the claim not to hold, this gives an adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
which is capable to distinguish
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
and
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{Q})
with non-negligible advantage. This adversary can be used to define an adversary
{\mathcal{B}}
with the same advantage that given on input
\mathcal{R}
, it first computes the continuation
t_{\mathtt{P}}
on the sample
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
and then feeds this value into
{\mathcal{A}}
. The advantage of
{\mathcal{B}}
is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
∎

Lemma 11
.

If
\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e
,
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
and
r\notin\mathsf{FV}(e)
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\llbracket r\rrbracket\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right)=\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})=\llbracket e\rrbracket\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right).

Proof.

We start by fixing
n\in\mathbb{N}
and by observing that:

\displaystyle\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\text{bind}(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}_{n}),m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m(r))})

the expression
\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}_{n})
can be expanded as follows:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(l[r\mapsto t])}))

using the associative property of the distribution monad and the property of
{\text{unit}(\cdot)}
, the whole expression can be rewritten as follows:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto\text{bind}({\text{unit}(l[r\mapsto t])},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m(r))}))).

The bind-unit pattern can be simplified as follows:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto{{\text{unit}(l[r\mapsto t](r))}})).

If we observe that
{l[r\mapsto t]}(r)=t
, it becomes

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto{{\text{unit}(t)}})),

which, in turn equals

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto{\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l)})=\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}).

With a similar chain of equivalences, it is possible to rewrite
\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right)
as follows::

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l[r\mapsto t]))).

With an application of Lemma
4
, we observe that this is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l))).

The assumptions of Lemma
4
can be matched by taking
\Xi
as the environment obtained removing
r
from
\Delta
. The inner bind can be expanded as:

\sum_{x\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{n}}\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(t)\cdot\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l)=1\cdot\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l).

For this reason the expression above can be rewritten as

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto{\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l)})=\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}).

∎

Lemma 12
.

For every
n
, every
\mathtt{P}
and every
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, we have

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)}))
.

Proof.

By induction on
\mathtt{P}
.

-

(
\mathtt{skip}
). In this case, the claim is
\mathcal{R}_{n}=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m)})
, which is trivial.

-

(
r\leftarrow e
). In this case, observe that:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)}))

is equal to:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}({\text{unit}(m)},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto l[r\mapsto t]))),

that, in turn, equals:

\displaystyle\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto m[r\mapsto t]))=\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})

-

(
\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}
). The claim is

\llbracket\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})).

For the IH on
\mathtt{P}
, we can rewrite
\llbracket\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
as follows:

\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})))

For the IH on
\mathtt{R}
, this claim can be rewritten again as:

\text{bind}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})),w\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(w)}))

That can be simplified as follows:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)}),w\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(w)})))

With the application of the IH on
\mathtt{R}
, we conclude.

-

(
\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}
). The claim is a direct consequence of the
definition of the semantics of the construct.

∎

Lemma 13
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and every
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}))
,
there is
\overline{m}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
such that

m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{m})}))
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on
\mathtt{P}
.

-

(
\mathtt{skip}
). Trivial.

-

(
r\leftarrow e
). Assume that the claim does not hold.
This means that there is
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}))
such that for every
l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
, we have:

\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(l[r\mapsto t])})(m)=0.

From this result we can deduce

\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})(m)=0
,
but this contradicts the premise.

-

(
\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}
). Assume that the claim does not hold.
This means there is
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}))
such that
for every
l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
, we have:

\displaystyle l(r)=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})(m)=0

\displaystyle l(r)=1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})(m)=0.

This shows that

\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})(m)=0
,
but this contradicts the premise.

-

(
\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}
). Assume
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}))
,
we must show that there is
\overline{m}
is
such that:

m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{m})})))

From the IH on
\mathtt{R}
, we deduce that there is
\overline{l}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n}))
such that

m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{l})})).

With another application of this hypothesis, we deduce that there is
\overline{w}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
such that:

\overline{l}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{w})})).

With an application of
Lemma

12
, we observe that

\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{w})}))(m)

is equal to:

\text{bind}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{w})}),l\mapsto\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)}))(m),

which in turn is equal to

\sum_{l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{w})}))}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{w})})(l)\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(l)})(m)

which is greater than:

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{w})})(\overline{l})\cdot\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(\overline{l})})(m)

which is greater than
0
for the assumptions above.

∎

Lemma 14
.

If
\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow d
,
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
and
r\notin\mathsf{FV}(d)
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\forall l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket r\leftarrow d\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})).\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(l)=\llparenthesis r\rrparenthesis_{n}(l).

Proof.

Assume this to be false for some
n
and some
l\in\llbracket r\leftarrow d\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
. From
Lemma

13
, we deduce that there is that there is
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}({\mathcal{R}})
such that
l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket r\leftarrow d\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)}))
.
Observe that:

\displaystyle\llbracket r\leftarrow d\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})

\displaystyle=\text{bind}({\text{unit}(m)},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket d\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])}))

\displaystyle=\text{bind}(\llbracket d\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])})

\displaystyle=\text{bind}({\text{unit}(\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m))},t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])})
