\displaystyle={\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)])}.

From this observation, we deduce that
l=m[r\mapsto\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)]
. In particular, we have that
\llparenthesis r\rrparenthesis_{n}(l)=l(r)=\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)
, and from
Remark

2
, we also conclude that
\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(l)=\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m[r\mapsto\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)])=\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)
, but this is absurd because we were assuming that
\llparenthesis r\rrparenthesis_{n}(l)\neq\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(l)
.
∎

Lemma 15
.

Let
\Delta,\Xi
and
\Theta

be environments such that:

•

\Delta\Join\Xi\downarrow
.

•

\Delta\vdash e:\tau
.

•

\Delta\Join\Xi\vdash r\leftarrow e
.

and let
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{Q}\in\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\rrbracket
,
such that

\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\Join\Xi\to\Delta}=\mathcal{Q}_{\Delta\Join\Xi\to\Delta}

it holds that

\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\Join\Xi\to\Delta\cup\{r:\tau\}}=\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{Q})_{\Delta\Join\Xi\to\Delta\cup\{r:\tau\}}
.

Proof.

We start by expanding
\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\Join\Xi\to\Delta\cup\{r:\tau\}}
as
\text{bind}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t])})),l\mapsto{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)\cup\{r\}})})
. This rewrites as follows:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m[r\mapsto t]_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)\cup\{r\}})})),

which is equal to:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}[r\mapsto t])})),

By
Lemma

4
, we deduce that, in turn, it equals:

\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}[r\mapsto t])})),

We can also observe that this is equal to:

\text{bind}({(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\Join\Xi\to\Delta})}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(l[r\mapsto t])}))

by expanding the definition of projection and applying standard properties of monads. From our premise, we deduce that it is equal to:

\text{bind}({(\mathcal{Q}_{\Delta\Join\Xi\to\Delta})}_{n},l\mapsto\text{bind}(\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(l),t\mapsto{\text{unit}(l[r\mapsto t])})).

By applying the same steps but in the reverse order, we are able to show the claim that we are aiming at.
∎

4
Cryptographic Probabilistic Separation Logic

This section is devoted to introducing a variation on
Barthe et al.’s probabilistic separation logic called

cryptographic probabilistic separation logic
(CPSL in the following).
Compared to PSL, but also to the logic of bunched
implications (BI)
[
24
,
25
]
, CPSL is simpler
in that the only connectives are multiplicative
and additive conjunctions, i.e.
*
and
\land
.
In addition, instead of working with plain, untyped formulas, we
work with
well-typed
formulas. Thanks to this choice, the semantics of
separating conjunction is more precise than in PSL,
and as a
byproduct gives rise to a lighter formulation of program logic rules
in Section
5
.

We first introduce the
syntax of CSL and its formulas,
then we proceed by defining a semantics for it
based on
efficiently samplable
randomized stores.
Finally, we show that CSL enjoys some basic but useful
properties.

4.1
The Syntax and Semantics of CSL

\displaystyle\llbracket{(\mathbf{U}(e))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket\mid

\displaystyle\;\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{(\mathbf{CI}(e,g))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket\mid

\displaystyle\;\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\approx\llbracket g\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{(\mathbf{EQ}(e,g))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket\mid

\displaystyle\;\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})=\llbracket g\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket\mid

\displaystyle\;\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\forall m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n}).\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis c\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)\}

s\models^{\Delta}{(A)^{\Delta}}

iff
s\in\llbracket{(A)^{\Delta}}\rrbracket

s\models^{\Delta}{(\top)^{\Delta}}

always

s\models^{\Delta}{(\bot)^{\Delta}}

never

s\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}

iff there are
r\sqsubseteq s,t\sqsubseteq s
such that
r\models^{\Xi}{(\phi)^{\Xi}}
and
t\models^{\Theta}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}

s\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}

iff there are
r,t
such that
r\otimes t\downarrow
,
r\otimes t\approx\sqsubseteq s
,
r\models^{\Xi}{(\phi)^{\Xi}}
and

t\models^{\Theta}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}

Figure 2:
Semantics of CSL

4.1.1
Syntax

We write
\mathcal{AP}
for the set of atomic formulas as defined in the following grammar:

\displaystyle A::=\mathbf{U}(e)\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\mathbf{CI}(e,e)\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\mathbf{EQ}(e,e)\;\mbox{\Large{$\mid$}}\;\mathbf{IS}(d,d)

The formula
\mathbf{U}(e)
, present in PSL, is interpreted differently here,
and indicates that the expression
e
evaluates to a distribution that is
computationally indistinguishable from the uniform one. The three binary atomic
formulas
\mathbf{CI}(e,g)
,
\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)
and
\mathbf{IS}(d,c)
all speak of indistinguishability between the two expressions
involved but at different levels, and their semantics will be explained in detail
later. We also define a type system for atomic formulas in an environment.
Judgments for this type system are in the form
\Delta\vdash A
, and
are proved valid by the following rules:

\displaystyle\Delta\vdash\mathbf{U}(e)\Delta\vdash e:\tau\qquad\qquad\Delta\vdash\mathbf{CI}(e,g)\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash e:\tau\Delta\vdash g:\tau

\displaystyle\Delta\vdash\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash e:\tau\Delta\vdash g:\tau\qquad\Delta\vdash\mathbf{IS}(d,c)\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash d:\tau\Delta\vdash c:\tau

On top of the set of atomic formulas, one can build the set of generic
formulas, as follows:

Definition 10
.

For a fixed environment
\Delta
, the set
{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}

of
formulas for
\Delta
is the smallest set of expressions closed under the following
rules:

\displaystyle{(A)^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}\Delta\vdash A\quad{(\top)^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}\quad{(\bot)^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}

\displaystyle{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}\lx@proof@logical@and{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Xi}}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Theta}}\Xi\Join\Theta\sqsubseteq\Delta

\displaystyle{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}\lx@proof@logical@and{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Xi}}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Theta}}\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta\Theta\sqsubseteq\Delta

The set of CSL formulas
\Phi
is the union of all the sets

{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}
over all possible environments
\Delta
.
∎

As already observed, the binary connectives we consider are just
conjunctions. On the other hand, formulas are typed and, crucially, the way in
which a separating conjunction splits the underlying environment is fixed by the
formula itself.

4.1.2
Semantics

The semantics of CSL formulas is a family of relations

\models^{\Delta}
such that, for every environment
\Delta
, we have that

\models^{\Delta}\subseteq{\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket}\times{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}
.
The full semantics is in Figure
2
where, in particular,
with
\approx\sqsubseteq
we denote the composition of
\approx
and
\sqsubseteq
.

The role of the three different forms of binary atomic formulas should be now clearer.
Atomic propositions like
{(\mathbf{CI}(e,g))^{\Delta}}
capture
computational indistinguishability:

{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models^{\Delta}{(\mathbf{CI}(e,g))^{\Delta}}
holds whenever

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})
is computationally
indistinguishable from

\llbracket\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})
.
The atomic formula
{(\mathbf{EQ}(e,g))^{\Delta}}
captures
a stronger form equivalence: it holds in a state
\mathcal{R}
if and only if

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket({\mathcal{R}})
and

\llbracket\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket({\mathcal{R}})
are equal —
instead of being simply indistinguishable. Finally,
{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Delta}}
states
something even stronger:

\mathcal{R}\models^{\Delta}{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Delta}}

holds if and only if for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, every sample
m

in the support of the distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket

is such that
\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash c:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)
.

The semantics of
\mathbf{IS}(d,c)
differs from that of

\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)
for two main reasons: first, the atomic proposition

\mathbf{IS}(d,c)
applies only to
deterministic
expressions,
while
\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)
applies also to
probabilistic
expressions.
Second, we have
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\llbracket{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket

when the
values
obtained by computing
d
and
c
on every sample of

{\mathsf{supp}}({\mathcal{R}_{n}})
are identical independently of the security parameter
n
;
instead,
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in{(\mathbf{EQ}(e,g))^{\Delta}}
requires the
families of distributions

obtained by evaluating
e
and
g
on the ensemble
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}

are the same.

Example 2
.

The distribution ensemble
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket r,s:\mathsf{Bool}\rrbracket

that is defined as follows

\mathcal{R}_{n}\mathrel{:=}\left\{\{r\mapsto 0,s\mapsto 1\}^{\frac{1}{2}},\{r\mapsto 1,s\mapsto 0\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}

satisfies
\mathbf{EQ}(r,s)
because the marginal distributions of

r
and
s
are identical, but it does not
satisfy
\mathbf{IS}(r,s)
because there is
a value of the security parameter
n

and a sample
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})

where
m(r)\neq m(s)
(actually this happens for every sample, independently of
n
). ∎

The interpretation of the additive conjunction is the same as in
PSL except that it asks that the sub-formulas hold in specific parts of
the stores. Formally, we have that
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\phi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}
whenever
there are
\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{R}
such that:

{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}\models^{\Xi}{(\phi)^{\Xi}}

and
{(\mathcal{T})^{\Theta}}\models^{\Theta}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}
.

The crux of CSL is the way separating conjunction is interpreted, which
now refers to computational independence: we ask that
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\phi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}

holds whenever it is possible to find two ensembles
\mathcal{Q}\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket

and
\mathcal{T}\in\llbracket\Theta\rrbracket

such that
{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}\models^{\Xi}{(\phi)^{\Xi}},{(\mathcal{T})^{\Theta}}\models^{\Theta}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}
, and
the projection of
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}
on the
environment
\Xi\Join\Theta
is indistinguishable
from the tensor product of
{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}
and

{(\mathcal{T})^{\Theta}}
.
As argued in
Section

7

below, the last condition is a characterization of the standard notion of
computational independence given by Fay
[
15
]
.

So, our interpretation of the separating conjunction — which is
based on that of BI — exactly captures computational independence.

Notational Conventions

In the following, we often omit
\Delta
from
\models^{\Delta}

if it is evident from the context, from
{(A)^{\Delta}}

when
A
mentions exactly the variables of
\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
,
And from

{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\odot{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}

for
\odot\in\{\land,*\}
when

\Delta
is exactly
\Xi\Join\Delta
.
In order to simplify the presentation of the formulas,
we write
\Delta_{S}
for the environment obtained by restricting
the domain of
\Delta
to
S
. We also omit
\Delta
when it is
clear from the context. In particular, when

s={(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}
and
s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}

holds, we write simply
s\models\phi
.
Finally, we write

{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\models^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}

as a shorthand for:

\forall s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left(s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow s\models^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}.

4.1.3
Algebraic Properties of Efficiently Samplable Distribution Ensembles

We model CSL on efficiently samplable distribution ensembles;
this set, in turn enjoys some remarkable algebraic properties with respect to
the tensor product
\mathrel{\otimes}
and to the relations
\approx

and
\sqsubseteq
. In the following, we review some of them
in order to prepare the ground for showing, in the
Section

4.4.2
,
that the structure
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\otimes},\mathbf{0},\approx\sqsubseteq)
is a
partial
Kripke resource monoid
(
Definition

11
). This result, in turn,
allows us to compare the semantics of our logic
with the standard interpretation the conjunctive fragment
of BI on
partial Kripke resource monoids

in
Propositions

2
and
3
.

Definition 11
.

A partial Kripke resource monoid
(M,\bullet,e,\leq)

consists of a set
M
together with a partial binary operation

\bullet
, an element
e\in M
and a pre-order
\leq

satisfying the following:

1.

For every
x\in M
, both
x\bullet e
and

e\bullet x
are defined, and equal to
x
.

2.

The operation
\bullet
is associative, in the following sense:

(x\bullet y)\bullet z
and

x\bullet(y\bullet z)
are either both undefined or
both defined, and in the latter case, they are equal.

3.

The pre-order
\leq
is compatible with
\bullet
, i.e.,
if
x\leq y
and

x\bullet z,y\bullet z
are both defined,
then
x\bullet z\leq y\bullet z
(and
similarly for
z\bullet x,z\bullet y
).
∎

Showing that the relation
\approx\sqsubseteq
, is a pre-order and is compatible with
respect to
\otimes
, as required by
Definition

11
, requires appreciable effort.

Properties of projection.

We start with the projection operation on stores. We start by observing that the projection
\cdot_{\Delta\to\Delta}
is the identity.

Remark 3

(Identity)
.

For every
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Delta}=\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Delta})_{n}=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})})=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m)})=\mathcal{R}.

∎

We also observe that projections are closed under composition, when this operation is defined.

Lemma 16

(Closure Under Composition of Projections)
.

Whenever
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{2}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{3}
, for every
\mathcal{R}_{3}\in\llbracket\Delta_{3}\rrbracket
, it holds that:

((\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{2}})_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}=(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}}

Proof.

((\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{2}})_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}

equals

n\mapsto\text{bind}(\text{bind}({\mathcal{R}_{3}}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{2})})}),m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{1})})})

which, in turn, equals:

n\mapsto\text{bind}({\mathcal{R}_{3}}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}({m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{2})}}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{1})})}).

Observe that
{m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{2})}}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{1})}=m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{1})}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Properties of
\sqsubseteq

The extension relation
\sqsubseteq
is defined on top of the notion of projection; for this reason,
Remark

3
and
Lemma

16
can be used to show that
\sqsubseteq
is a pre-order.

Lemma 17

(Pre-order)
.

The relation
\sqsubseteq
is a pre-order relation on
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

•

For reflexivity, we need to show that

{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\sqsubseteq{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}.

In particular, this requires to verify that
\Delta\sqsubseteq\Delta
and that

\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Delta}=\mathcal{R}
, that is
Remark

3
.

•

For transitivity, we need to show that:

\displaystyle{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}\sqsubseteq{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}.

The conclusion on the environments is a consequence of the transitivity of
\subseteq
.
Then we need to show that

(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{1}
. We also know that

(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{2}}=\mathcal{R}_{2}
and that

(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{1}
; finally,
due to
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{2}
, we also know that
\left((\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{2}}\right)_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}=(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}}
. Therefore:

\mathcal{R}_{1}=(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}=((\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{2}})_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}=(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{1}}.

The last equivalence is a consequence of Lemma
16
.

∎

Properties of
\approx
.

We start by showing that
\approx
is an equivalence relation (Lemma
18
). This observation is crucial for showing that the composition of
\approx
and
\sqsubseteq
is a pre-order. Then, we show that
\approx
is compatible with the tensor product (
Lemma

19
), and that
\approx
and
\sqsubseteq
enjoy a form of distributivity (
Lemma

20
). These properties are respectively used to show that the composition of
\approx
and
\sqsubseteq
is compatible with the tensor product, and that it is transitive, and these are both crucial properties for showing that
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\otimes},\mathbf{0},\approx\sqsubseteq)
is a
partial
Kripke resource monoid
.

Lemma 18
.

The relation
\approx
is an equivalence on
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

•

Reflexivity is trivial.

•

For symmetry we need to show that:

\displaystyle{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Delta}}}\Rightarrow{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Delta}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}},

which is a consequence of the fact that
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
every polytime distinguisher

{\mathcal{A}}:\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\to\llbracket\mathbb{B}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
:

\displaystyle\left|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow\mathcal{Q}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]\right|=

\displaystyle\left|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow\mathcal{Q}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]\right|.

•

For transitivity we need to show that:

\displaystyle{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta}}\approx{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta}}\land{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta}}\approx{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta}}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta}}\approx{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta}}.

It holds that:

\displaystyle\left|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{1}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{3}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(n,m)=1\right]\right|=

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\big|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{1}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{3}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(n,m)=1\right]+\\
{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(n,m)=1\right]\big|\leq\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\left|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{1}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(n,m)=1\right]\right|+\\
\left|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{3}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{2}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(n,m)=1\right]\right|\leq\end{multlined}

\displaystyle{\mathit{negl}}_{1}(n)+{\mathit{negl}}_{2}(n)

We know that
{\mathit{negl}}_{1}
and
{\mathit{negl}}_{2}
are two negligible functions because of the
indistinguishability of
{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta}}
and
{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta}}

and that of
{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta}}
and
{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta}}
.
The sum of two negligible functions is a negligible function.

∎

Lemma 19

(Compatibility of
\approx
and
\otimes
)
.

The relation
\approx
and the function
\otimes
are compatible, i.e.

\forall s,r,t.s\approx r\Rightarrow(s\otimes t)\downarrow\Rightarrow(r\otimes t)\downarrow\Rightarrow(s\otimes t)\approx(r\otimes t)

and

\forall s,r,t.s\approx r\Rightarrow(t\otimes s)\downarrow\Rightarrow(t\otimes r)\downarrow\Rightarrow(t\otimes s)\approx(t\otimes r)

Proof.

We only show the right compatibility. The proof of left compatibility is analogous. Assume the statement not to hold, i.e. there are three stores
s={{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}},r={{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}},t={{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}}
such that
s\approx r
,
(s\otimes t)
is defined,
(r\otimes t)
is defined and finally that there is a distinguisher
{\mathcal{A}}
which distinguishes
s\otimes t
and
s\otimes r
with non-negligible advantage.
We know that
\mathcal{R}_{3}
is efficiently samplable for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, thus, we can call
\mathit{R}
the probabilistic polytime algorithm sampling
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{n}
. In particular, the distinguisher
{\mathcal{B}}
can be obtained by sampling from the poly-time algorithm for
\mathcal{R}_{3}
, namely
\mathit{R}
to obtain a sample
l
. Then,
{\mathcal{B}}
combines its input sample
m
and
l
in a unique sample and then invokes
{\mathcal{A}}
on this sample. By polynomiality of
{\mathcal{A}}
and of
\mathit{R}
,
{\mathcal{B}}
is polynomial. Moreover, the advantage of
{\mathcal{B}}
in distinguishing
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}
on
(\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{R}_{3})
and
(\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{R}_{3})
.
∎

Lemma 20

(Distributivity of
\approx
and
\sqsubseteq
)
.

If
s\sqsubseteq r\approx t
, then there exists
u
such that

s\approx u\sqsubseteq t
.

Proof.

The claim we are aiming to show is:
for every
s,r,t\in\mathcal{S}
,

s\sqsubseteq r\approx t
, then there is
u\in\mathcal{S}
such that

s\approx u\sqsubseteq t
. This claim can be equivalently restated as follows:

\displaystyle{{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}}\sqsubseteq{{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\exists\Delta_{4},\mathcal{R}_{4}.{{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{R}_{4})^{\Delta_{4}}}}\sqsubseteq{{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}}

Choosing
\Delta_{4}=\Delta_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{4}=(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}}
,
we are able to show the claim.

•

To show
\Delta_{4}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{3}
, we can show that
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{3}
. From our assumption, we know that
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{3}
, so
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{3}
.

•

Similarly, to show
\mathcal{R}_{4}=(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{4}}
it suffices to observe that
\Delta_{4}=\Delta_{1}
.

This concludes the proof that
{{(\mathcal{R}_{4})^{\Delta_{4}}}}\sqsubseteq{{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}}
. It remains to show that:

{{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}}\approx{((\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}})^{\Delta_{1}}}

(
*
)

Assume that
(\mathcal{R}_{s})
and
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}}
are distinguishable by
{\mathcal{A}}
, from Definition
7
, this is equivalent to assuming that:

\left|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{\mathcal{R}_{1}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}}}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]\right|

is not a negligible function. Let the distinguisher
{\mathcal{B}}
which take its input
1^{n}
and a sample
l\in\llbracket\Delta_{3}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
and executes
{\mathcal{A}}
on
(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{1})},1^{n})
.
{\mathcal{B}}
is still a probabilistic polynomial time computable function. Due to
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{3}
, the probability of returning 1 of
{\mathcal{B}}
on samples taken from
\mathcal{R}_{3}
is exactly that of
{\mathcal{A}}
on samples taken from
(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, the output distribution is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}
on
(\mathcal{R}_{2})_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{1}
. For this reason:

\left|{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{(\mathcal{R}_{3})}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{B}}(1^{n},m)=1\right]-{\text{Pr}_{m\leftarrow{(\mathcal{R}_{2})}_{n}}}\left[{\mathcal{B}}(1^{n},(\mathcal{R}_{2})(n))=1\right]\right|

is not a negligible function, but this is absurd since we were assuming that:

{{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}},

so
{\mathcal{A}}
cannot exist, and thus
(*)
holds.
∎

Compatibility of
\sqsubseteq
and
\approx
.

The last property that we need to show is the compatibility of
\sqsubseteq
and
\approx
. With
Lemma

19
, this property is useful to show that even the relation
\approx\sqsubseteq
is compatible with
\mathrel{\otimes}
, and enabling us to show that
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\otimes},\mathbf{0},\approx\sqsubseteq)
is a
partial Kripke resource monoid
. The compatibility of
\sqsubseteq
and
\approx
is shown in
Lemma

22
below, and it relies on showing that the projection is distributive with the tensor product (
Lemma

21
).

Lemma 21

(Distributivity of Product over Restriction)
.

Whenever
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
\mathcal{Q}\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
,
\Theta\sqsubseteq\Delta,\Omega\sqsubseteq\Xi
, and
\Xi\Join\Delta\downarrow
, it holds that:

({(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}\mathrel{\otimes}{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}})_{\Xi\Join\Delta\to(\Theta\Join\Omega)}=({{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}_{{\Xi}\to{\Theta}}\mathrel{\otimes}{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}_{{\Delta}\to{\Omega}}).

Proof.

Showing the identity of the final environments is trivial, so we will focus only on the distribution ensembles. We start by expanding the definition of
({(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}\mathrel{\otimes}{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}})_{\Xi\Join\Delta\to(\Theta\Join\Omega)}
for a given
n
:

\displaystyle=\text{bind}((\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{Q})_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta\Join\Omega)})})

\displaystyle=u\mapsto\;\;\sum_{m\in\llbracket\Xi\Join\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}}\;\;{\mathcal{Q}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)})\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})}\cdot{{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta\Join\Omega)})}}(u)

\displaystyle=u\mapsto\;\;\sum_{l\in\llbracket{\Xi}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}},w\in\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}}\;\;{\mathcal{Q}_{n}(l)\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}(w)}\cdot{{\text{unit}((l\cup w)_{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta\Join\Omega)})}}(u)

\displaystyle=u\mapsto\;\;\sum_{l\in\llbracket{\Xi}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}},w\in\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}}\;\;{\mathcal{Q}_{n}(l)\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}(w)}\cdot{{\text{unit}((l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)}\cup w_{\mathsf{dom}(\Omega)}))}}(u)

Observe that
{{\text{unit}((l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)}\cup w_{\mathsf{dom}(\Omega)}))}}(u)
is equal to:

{{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)})}(u_{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)})\cdot{\text{unit}(w_{\mathsf{dom}(\Omega)})}}(u_{\mathsf{dom}(\Omega)})

From this observation, the conclusion comes easily, by associativity property and closing back the definition of projection.
∎

Lemma 22

(Compatibility of
\sqsubseteq
and
\otimes
)
.

The relation
\sqsubseteq
and the function
\otimes
are compatible, i.e.

\forall s,r,t.s\sqsubseteq r\Rightarrow(s\otimes t)\downarrow\Rightarrow(r\otimes t)\downarrow\Rightarrow(s\otimes t)\sqsubseteq(r\otimes t)

and

\forall s,r,t.s\sqsubseteq r\Rightarrow(t\otimes s)\downarrow\Rightarrow(t\otimes r)\downarrow\Rightarrow(t\otimes s)\sqsubseteq(t\otimes r)

Proof.

We only show the right compatibility, the proof of left compatibility is analogous. Assume that there are three stores
s={{(\mathcal{R}_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}},r={{(\mathcal{R}_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}},t={{(\mathcal{R}_{3})^{\Delta_{3}}}}
such that
s\sqsubseteq r
,
(s\otimes t)
is defined, and
(r\otimes t)
is defined. We want to show that:

\Delta_{1}\Join\Delta_{3}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{2}\Join\Delta_{3},

(Claim 1)

and that:

\left(\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{R}_{3}\right)_{\Delta_{2}\Join\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}\Join\Delta_{3}}=\mathcal{R}_{1}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{R}_{3}.

(Claim 2)

Claim 1 is trivial. Claim 2 is a consequence of Lemma
21
, which allows us to show:

\left(\mathcal{R}_{2}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{R}_{3}\right)_{\Delta_{2}\Join\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{1}\Join\Delta_{3}}={(\mathcal{R}_{1})}_{\Delta_{2}\to\Delta_{1}}\mathrel{\otimes}(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{3}}.

By assumption we know that
\left(\mathcal{R}_{2}\right)_{{\Delta_{2}}\to{\Delta_{1}}}=\mathcal{R}_{1}
, from Lemma
17
we conclude that

(\mathcal{R}_{3})_{\Delta_{3}\to\Delta_{3}}=\mathcal{R}_{3}.

∎

4.2
Some Relevant Classes of Formulas

Some properties of CSL we
prove
in Section
4.3
below only hold for specific classes of formulas
within
\Phi
, which need to be appropriately defined.
We start by the notion of
exact formula
:
intuitively, these formulas describe

statistical
properties of distributions
rather than
computational
ones.

We write
{\Phi_{\mathsf{ex}}}
for the set of exact formulas, i.e., the smallest
subset of
\Phi
that for every
\Delta
contains
the formulas
{(\top)^{\Delta}}
,

{(\bot)^{\Delta}}
,
{(\mathbf{EQ}(e,g))^{\Delta}}
,

{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Delta}}
and
which is closed under additive conjunction. This means that
if
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
and
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}

are exact,

\Delta\sqsubseteq\Theta
and
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Theta
,
then
{({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\land{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Theta}}

is also exact.

Crucially, the semantics of exact formulas is not
closed with respect to
\approx
, as shown by the following example:

Example 3
.

The stores
\mathcal{R}=\left\{\left\{(r\mapsto 0^{n})^{1}\right\}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

and
\mathcal{Q}=\left\{\left\{(r\mapsto 0^{n})^{1-2^{-n}},(r\mapsto 1^{n})^{2^{-n}}\right\}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

are computationally indistinguishable, but they do not satisfy the same formulas.

In particular, we have
\mathcal{R}\models\mathbf{EQ}(\mathit{setzero}_{n},r)
,
but not

\mathcal{Q}\models\mathbf{EQ}(\mathit{setzero}_{n},r)
. More precisely, the distribution
corresponding to the expression
{\mathit{setzero}_{n}}
is the Dirac distribution
centered on
0^{n}
, which is exactly the same distribution obtained by evaluating

r
in
\mathcal{R}
, but not in
\mathcal{Q}
.
∎

Also notice that, in the previous example,
if we took
{\mathbf{CI}(\mathit{setzero}_{n},r)}
instead of
{\mathbf{EQ}(\mathit{setzero}_{n},r)}
,
we would have
\mathcal{R}\models\mathbf{CI}(\mathit{setzero}_{n},r)
,
and

\mathcal{Q}\models\mathbf{CI}(\mathit{setzero}_{n},r)
. Specifically, this would hold because
the advantage of any distinguisher for the family of distributions

\{\llbracket\mathit{setzero}_{n}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R}_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

and
\{\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is 0, and
that of every adversary for the same two expressions evaluated in
\mathcal{Q}

is bounded by
2^{-n}
, which is a negligible function.
Unlike
exact formulas
, formulas like
\mathbf{CI}(\cdot,\cdot)

do not describe exact statistical properties
of distribution ensembles, but they express computational
properties. For this reason, we call these formulas

approximate
.
The set of
approximate formulas
is
{\Phi_{\mathsf{ap}}}
, and it is defined as the largest subset of
\Phi
where the atomic formulas

\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)
and

\mathbf{IS}(d,c)
do not occur as subformulas.
Due to their computational nature, the semantics of

approximate formulas
is closed with respect to
\approx
.
This is shown in
Lemma

24
below.

4.3
Properties of CSL

In this section, we discuss the meta-theoretical
properties of CSL. In particular its semantics
enjoys some remarkable closure properties. The most important
is a form of monotonicity with respect to
\sqsubseteq
. Intuitively,
this property states that when two states
s,r

are such that
s\sqsubseteq r
,

s
and
r
satisfy
related
formulas.
It is not true that they satisfy
the
same
set of formulas, simply because
our semantics
relates states and formulas
on the same environment
,
so states that are defined on different domains
cannot be in relation with the same formulas.
However, we can say that whenever two states
s,r

are such that
s\sqsubseteq r
and
s

satisfies
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
, then
r

satisfies a formula
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}

which is structurally identical to

{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
, but which carries different environments.
This relation between formulas is induced by the
store extension relation
\sqsubseteq
as follows:

{(A)^{\Delta}}\sqsubseteq{(A)^{\Xi}}\Delta\sqsubseteq\Xi{}\quad{({(\phi)^{\Delta}}*{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Theta_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{({(\phi)^{\Delta}}*{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Theta_{2}}}{\Theta_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\Theta_{2}}

{({(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\land{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}})^{\Theta_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{({(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}\land{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}})^{\Theta_{2}}}\lx@proof@logical@and{(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}}\Theta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Theta_{2}

By leveraging this relation, we can state
the property we were discussing above as follows:

Lemma 23
.

For every pair of environments
\Delta,\Xi
, pair of formulas
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi)^{\Xi}}{}
and pair of states
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket,r\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
such that
s\sqsubseteq r
, it holds that
s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\Leftrightarrow r\models^{\Xi}{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
.
∎

The following observations turn out to be useful in the proof of
Lemma

23
:

Remark 4
.

{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\Rightarrow\Delta\sqsubseteq\Xi
.

Remark 5
.

If two distribution ensembles
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket,\mathcal{Q}\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
are such that
\mathcal{R}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{Q}
,
then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})=\{m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\mid\exists l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q}).l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}=m\}
.

Proof.

From
\mathcal{R}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{Q}
, we deduce that
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{R}_{n}=\text{bind}(\mathcal{Q}_{n},m\mapsto m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})
,
so the claim becomes

{\mathsf{supp}}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{Q}_{n},m\mapsto{\text{unit}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})}))=\\
\{m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\mid\exists l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q}).m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}=m\},

by definition of
{\mathsf{supp}}(\cdot)
, this is equivalent to:

\left\{m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\mid\;\;\sum_{l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q})}\;\;\mathcal{Q}_{n}(l)\cdot{\text{unit}(l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})}(m)>0\right\}=\\
\{m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\mid\exists l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q}).l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}=m\}.

In turn, this rewrites as follows:

\left\{m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\mid\;\;\;\;\sum_{l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q})\land l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}=m}\;\;\;\;\mathcal{Q}_{n}(l)>0\right\}=\\
\{m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}\mid\exists l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q}).l_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}=m\}.

The equality of these expressions is trivial.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

23
.

By induction on the proof that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi)^{\Xi}}{}
.

•

If these are atomic formulas, we go by cases on the atomic proposition. We start by assuming that the proposition is
\mathbf{U}(e)
, and we start from the left-to-right implication. We assume
s\models^{\Delta}{(\mathbf{U}(e))^{\Delta}}
, which means that
\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(s){}\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)
. From
s\sqsubseteq r
and
Lemma

5
, we deduce that
\llbracket\Xi\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(r){}=\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(s){}\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)
, which concludes the proof. For the opposite direction, if we assume
\llbracket\Xi\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(r){}\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)
, we can apply
Lemma

5
and conclude that
\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(s){}=\llbracket\Xi\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(r){}\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)
.
If the proposition is
\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)
, from
s\sqsubseteq r
and
Lemma

5
, we deduce that
\llbracket\Xi\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(r){}=\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(s){}
and that
\llbracket\Xi\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket(r){}=\llbracket\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket(s){}
, so in both the directions the conclusion is a consequence of the transitivity of identity.
The case where the proposition is
\mathbf{CI}(e,g)
is analogous, but applying the transitivity of
\approx
(
Lemma

18
).
Finally, we assume that the proposition is
\mathbf{IS}(d,c)
. For the left-to-right implication, we assume that
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
and
\mathcal{Q}\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
with
\mathcal{R}\models{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Delta}}
and
\mathcal{R}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{Q}
. The goal is to show
\mathcal{Q}\models{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Xi}}
. Assume this not to hold. This means that there are
\overline{n}
and
\overline{m}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{n}})
such that
\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{\overline{n}}(\overline{m})\neq\llparenthesis c\rrparenthesis_{\overline{n}}(\overline{m})
. Then, we apply
Remark

5
to observe that
\overline{m}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R})
. From
Lemma

3
, we deduce
\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{\overline{n}}(\overline{m}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})\neq\llparenthesis c\rrparenthesis_{\overline{n}}(\overline{m}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})
, which contradicts the premise
\mathcal{R}\models{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Delta}}
. For the opposite direction, we assume that
\mathcal{Q}\models{(\mathbf{IS}(d,c))^{\Xi}}
. To this aim, we fix
n
, and we let
\overline{m}
be an element of
{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
. The goal is to show that we have
\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(\overline{m})=\llparenthesis c\rrparenthesis_{n}(\overline{m})
. From
Remark

5
, we deduce that there is
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q}_{n})
such that
\overline{m}=m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}
. From the assumption, we deduce that
\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis c\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)
, so with
Lemma

3
, we conclude that
\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})=\llparenthesis c\rrparenthesis_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)})
, which is equivalent to the claim because we assumed that
\overline{m}=m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}
.

•

If these are
{(\top)^{\Delta}}
or
{(\bot)^{\Delta}}
, the conclusion is trivial.

•

If the two formulas are additive conjunctions, then we start from the left to right implication. Assume
s\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\land{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}})^{\Delta}}
, so we deduce that
s_{1}\models^{\Delta_{1}}{(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}
and
s_{2}\models^{\Xi_{1}}{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}}
with
s_{1}=s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{1}}=(r_{\Xi\to\Delta})_{\Delta\to\Delta_{1}}=r_{\Xi\to\Delta_{1}}
, and
s_{2}=s_{\Delta\to\Xi_{1}}=(r_{\Xi\to\Delta})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{1}}=r_{\Xi\to\Xi_{1}}
by definition of
\sqsubseteq
and
Lemma

16
. From
{(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}
and
{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}}
, we can conclude that
\Xi_{1}\sqsubseteq\Xi_{2}
and that
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{2}
using
Remark

4
. For this reason, we can apply the IHs to the rule’s premises, to conclude
r_{\Xi\to\Delta_{2}}\models^{\Delta_{2}}{(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}
and
r_{\Xi\to\Xi_{2}}\models^{\Xi_{2}}{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}}
, which concludes the proof.
For the opposite direction, we assume that
r\models^{\Xi}{({(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}\land{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}})^{\Xi}}
, By definition of the semantic relation and by definition of
\sqsubseteq
, we deduce that:
r_{\Xi\to\Delta_{2}}\models^{\Delta_{2}}{(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}
and
r_{\Xi\to\Xi_{2}}\models^{\Xi_{2}}{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}}
. From
{{({(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\land{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}})^{\Delta}}}\sqsubseteq{{({(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}\land{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}})^{\Xi}}}
, we deduce that
{(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\phi_{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}
and
{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi_{2})^{\Xi_{2}}}
, so we can conclude that
\Xi_{1}\sqsubseteq\Xi_{2}
and that
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta_{2}
,
Remark

4
. These observations allow us to apply the IHs on the pair of states
(r_{\Xi\to\Delta_{2}},r_{\Xi\to\Delta_{1}})
, and
(r_{\Xi\to\Xi_{2}},r_{\Xi\to\Xi_{1}})
, to show that
r_{\Xi\to\Delta_{1}}\models^{\Delta_{1}}{(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}
and
r_{\Xi\to\Xi_{1}}\models^{\Xi_{1}}{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}}
. This also shows that
s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{1}}\models^{\Delta_{1}}{(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}}
and
s_{\Delta\to\Xi_{1}}\models^{\Xi_{1}}{(\psi_{1})^{\Xi_{1}}}
, because
r_{\Xi\to\Delta_{1}}=(r_{\Xi\to\Delta})_{\Delta\to\Delta_{1}}=s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{1}}
and
r_{\Xi\to\Xi_{1}}=(r_{\Xi\to\Delta})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{1}}=s_{\Delta\to\Xi_{1}}
, for
Lemma

16
. This concludes the proof.

•

If the two formulas are two separating conjunctions, then we start from the left to right implication. Assume
s\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}*{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}})^{\Delta}}
, so we deduce that
s_{1}\models^{\Theta}{(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}
and
s_{2}\models^{\Omega}{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}}
with
s_{1}\otimes s_{2}\approx\sqsubseteq s\sqsubseteq r
. So, we conclude
r\models^{\Xi}{({(\phi)^{\Theta}}*{(\psi)^{\Omega}})^{\Xi}}
. For the opposite direction, we assume that there are
r_{1}\models^{\Theta}{(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}
and
r_{2}\models^{\Omega}{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}}
with

r_{1}\otimes r_{2}\approx\sqsubseteq r
.
From the last observation and the definitions of
\approx
and
\sqsubseteq
, we can deduce that

r_{\Xi\to\Theta\Join\Omega}\approx r_{1}\otimes r_{2}
. We also know that
{{({(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}*{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}})^{\Delta}}}
is a formula, which means that
\Theta\Join\Omega\sqsubseteq\Delta
. So, by applying Lemma
16
we can conclude that
r_{1}\otimes r_{2}\approx r_{\Xi\to\Theta\Join\Omega}=(r_{\Xi\to\Delta})_{\Delta\to\Theta\Join\Omega}=(s)_{\Delta\to\Theta\Join\Omega}
which concludes the proof.

∎

The approximate fragment of CSL also enjoys good properties with respect to

\approx
.
In particular, if two states
s
and
r
are cryptographically indistinguishable,
and one of these satisfies a formula
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\in{\Phi_{\mathsf{ap}}}
,
then also the other satisfies
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
. This is stated in
Lemma

24
.

Lemma 24
.

For every environment
\Delta
, and every
approximate formula

{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\in{\Phi_{\mathsf{ap}}}
and pair of states
s,r\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
such that
s\approx r
, it holds that
s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\Rightarrow r\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

24
.

By induction on the proof that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
is a formula.

•

If
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
is an atomic formula, we go by cases on the atomic proposition. Now assume that the formula is
{(\mathbf{U}(e))^{\Delta}}
. We assume
s\models^{\Delta}{(\mathbf{U}(e))^{\Delta}}
, which means that
\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(s){}\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)
. From
s\approx r
and
Lemma

2
, we deduce that
\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(r){}\approx\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(s){}\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)
, which concludes the proof.

•

If
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
is
{(\top)^{\Delta}}
or
{(\bot)^{\Delta}}
, the conclusion is trivial.

•

If the formula is an additive conjunction, we assume
s\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}\land{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}})^{\Delta}}
, so we deduce that
s_{1}\models^{\Theta}{(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}
and
s_{2}\models^{\Omega}{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}}
with
s_{1}\sqsubseteq s
and
s_{2}\sqsubseteq s
. From this result, we deduce that
s_{\Delta\to\Theta}=s_{1}
and
s_{\Delta\to\Omega}=s_{2}
By applying
Lemma

2
, we conclude
r_{\Delta\to\Theta}\approx s_{1}
and
r_{\Delta\to\Omega}\approx s_{2}
. By applying the IHs to
s_{1}
and
r_{\Delta\to\Theta}
, and to
s_{2}
and
r_{\Delta\to\Omega}
, we are able to show that
r_{\Delta\to\Theta}\models^{\Theta}{(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}
and
r_{\Delta\to\Theta}\models^{\Omega}{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}}
, which concludes the proof.

•

If the formula is a separating conjunction, we assume
s\models^{\Delta}{({(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}*{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}})^{\Delta}}
, so we deduce that
s_{1}\models^{\Theta}{(\phi_{1})^{\Theta}}
and
s_{2}\models^{\Omega}{(\psi_{1})^{\Omega}}
with
s_{1}\otimes s_{2}\approx\sqsubseteq s\approx r
. By applying
Lemma

20
, we obtain
s_{1}\otimes s_{2}\approx\sqsubseteq r
, which concludes the proof.

∎

These two results, taken together, can be combined
in a third result which is analogous to the restriction
property of
[
17
]
:

Corollary 1
.

For every pair of environments
\Delta,\Xi
, every pair of approximate formulas
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
and
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
such that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi)^{\Xi}}{}
and every pair of states
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket,r\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
such that
s\approx\sqsubseteq r
, it holds that
s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\Leftrightarrow r\models^{\Xi}{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
.

We also introduce a sound although incomplete
set of axioms about atomic formulas. Despite their simplicity,
they are expressive enough to reason about cryptographic primitives.

Lemma 25
.

The following axiom schemas are valid:

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{CI}(e,e)

(S0)

\displaystyle\mathbf{CI}(e,g)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{CI}(g,e)

(S1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{CI}(e,g)\land\mathbf{CI}(g,h)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{CI}(e,h)

(S2)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{EQ}(e,e)

(T0)

\displaystyle\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{EQ}(g,e)

(T1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)\land\mathbf{EQ}(g,h)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{EQ}(e,h)

(T2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{CI}(e,g)

(W1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{IS}(d,c)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{EQ}(d,c)

(W2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{CI}(e,g)\land\mathbf{U}(e)

\displaystyle\models\mathbf{U}(g)

(U1)

∎

Proof of Lemma
25
.

-

(S0). Follows from reflexivity of
\approx
.

-

(S1). Follows from symmetry of
\approx
.

-

(S2). Follows from transitivity of
\approx
.

-

(T0-2). The axioms on
\mathbf{EQ}(\cdot,\cdot)
are trivial consequences of the fact that identity is an equation relation. Their proof would be similar to the correspondent axiom for
\mathbf{CI}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

-

(W1). Assume
\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})=\llbracket\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
, then:

\Pr_{t\leftarrow\llbracket e\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R})}[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},t)=1]=\Pr_{t\leftarrow\llbracket g\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{R})}[{\mathcal{A}}(1^{n},t)=1],

so the advantage of any adversary is exactly
0
.

-

(W2). Assume
\forall m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}).\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrparenthesis(m)=\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrparenthesis(m)
, then the goal is to show that
\llbracket\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})=\llbracket\Delta\vdash c:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
.
We fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The proof proceeds by expanding
\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\llbracket d\rrbracket^{\mathtt{d}}(m))(t)
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot\llbracket d\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(m))}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot{\text{unit}(\llparenthesis c\rrparenthesis_{n}(m))}(t)=\sum_{m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)\cdot\llbracket c\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}(m)(t).

-

(U1). Let
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}
be any store such that
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\models{\mathbf{CI}(x,y)}\land\mathbf{U}(y)
. The claim is
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\models\mathbf{U}(x)
. It holds that
\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\approx\llbracket\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R}){}\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)
. The claim follows from transitivity of
\approx
.

∎

{(A)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{(A)^{\Delta}}\quad\vdash^{\Delta}{(\top)^{\Delta}}\quad{(\bot)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}

{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\lx@proof@logical@and{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\sqsubseteq{(\phi)^{\Delta}}{(\xi)^{\Theta}}\sqsubseteq{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\quad{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{(\phi_{i})^{\Delta}}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{({{(\phi_{1})^{\Xi}}}\land{{(\phi_{2})^{\Theta}}})^{\Delta}}

{({{(\phi)^{\Xi}}}*{{(\psi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{({{(\xi)^{\Xi}}}*{{(\eta)^{\Theta}}})^{\Delta}}\lx@proof@logical@and{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\vdash^{\Xi}{(\xi)^{\Xi}}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}\vdash^{\Theta}{(\eta)^{\Theta}}\quad{({{(\phi)^{\Xi}}}*{{(\psi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{({{(\psi)^{\Theta}}}*{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}})^{\Delta}}

{(({{(\phi)^{\Xi}}}*{{(\psi)^{\Theta}}})*{(\xi)^{\Omega}})^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*({(\psi)^{\Theta}}*{(\xi)^{\Omega}}))^{\Delta}}

{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*({(\psi)^{\Theta}}*{(\xi)^{\Omega}}))^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{(({{(\phi)^{\Xi}}}*{{(\psi)^{\Theta}}})*{(\xi)^{\Omega}})^{\Delta}}

Figure 3:
A Hilbert-style proof system for CSL.

The two conjunctions of CSL are commutative
and enjoy a form of associativity, in particular, the
separating conjunction is associative when the environments
associated to a formula are minimal.

Lemma 26
.

For every
\odot\in\{\land,*\}
,
if
{({({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\odot{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}\odot{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta}}
is a formula, then

{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}\odot{(\phi)^{\Delta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}

and
{({{(\phi)^{\Delta}}}\odot{({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}\odot{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta}}
are also
formulas, and we have:

\displaystyle{{({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\odot{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}}

\displaystyle\models{{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}\odot{(\phi)^{\Delta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}}

(Commutativity)

\displaystyle{({({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\odot{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}\odot{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta}}

\displaystyle\models({{(\phi)^{\Delta}}}\odot({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}\odot{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta})^{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta}

(Associativity)

Proof.

The commutative property is trivial for both the conjunctions.
For the associative property we start by showing that

{({{(\phi)^{\Delta}}}\odot{({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}\odot{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta}}

is a formula. On the one hand, if
\odot=\land
,
we know that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
,

{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
are all formulas.
From this observation, we can conclude that

{({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}\land{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}

is a formula because
\Xi,\Theta\sqsubseteq\Xi\Join\Theta

and with a similar argument we show that the formula on the right is well-formed.
On the other hand, if
\odot=*
, we start by observing that
it must be the case where the domains of
\Delta,\Xi
and

\Theta
are disjoint, and as above
we know that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
,

{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
are formulas.
This means that

{({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}*{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}

is a formula, and with an argument, we have shown that the formula on the right is well-formed
also when the
\odot=*
.
Now we show that

{({({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\odot{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}\odot{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta}}\models{({{(\phi)^{\Delta}}}\odot{({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}\odot{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta}}

If
\odot=\land
, we assume that there is
s
which satisfies the premise.
This means that:

-

(H1).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Delta\Join\Xi}\models{({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\land{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}
, which in turn means that:

-

(H1A).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Delta}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.

-

(H1B).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Xi}\models{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
.

-

(H2).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Theta}\models{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
.

The claim asks us to show that:

-

(C1).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Delta}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.

-

(C2).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Xi\Join\Theta}\models{({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}\land{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}
, which can be reduced to showing:

-

(C2A).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Xi}\models{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
.

-

(C2B).
s_{\Delta\Join\Xi\Join\Theta\to\Theta}\models{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
.

by definition of the semantics of
\land

The conclusion is trivial, because the premises coincide with the claims.
On the other hand,
\odot=*
, the proof is more articulated.
We start by assuming that there is a state
s
which satisfies the premises.
This means that there are two states
r\in\llbracket\Delta\Join\Xi\rrbracket
,
t\in\llbracket\Theta\rrbracket
such that:

-

(H1).
r\models{({(\phi)^{\Delta}}*{(\psi)^{\Xi}})^{\Delta\Join\Xi}}
, which in turn means that there are two states
r_{1}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
and
r_{2}\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
such that:

-

(H1A).
r_{1}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.

-

(H1B).
r_{2}\models{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
.

-

(H1C).
r_{1}\otimes r_{2}\approx r
.

-

(H2).
t\models{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
.

-

(H3).
r\otimes t\approx s
.

The claim asks us to show that there are two states
u_{1}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket

and
u_{2}\in\llbracket\Xi\Join\Theta\rrbracket
such that :

-

(C1).
u_{1}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.

-

(C2).
u_{2}\models{({{(\psi)^{\Xi}}}*{{(\xi)^{\Theta}}})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}
.

-

(C3).
{u_{1}}\otimes u_{2}\approx s
.

We choose
u_{1}=r_{1}
and
u_{2}=r_{2}\otimes t
. With this
choice (C1) is trivial, (C2) requires showing that there are
u_{2a}\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket

and
u_{2b}\in\llbracket\Theta\rrbracket
such that:

-

(C2A).
u_{2a}\models{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
.

-

(C2B).
u_{2b}\models{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
.

-

(C2C).
u_{2a}\otimes u_{2b}\approx u_{2}
.

In particular, we can choose
u_{2a}=r_{2}
and
u_{2b}=t
.
With this choice (C2A) corresponds with (H1B),
(C2B) corresponds with (H2).
And (C2C) is trivial, because we can show:

u_{2a}\otimes u_{2b}=u_{2}.

In order to show (C3), we are required to show:

{u_{1}}\otimes u_{2}\approx s,

which means showing:

{r_{1}}\otimes(r_{2}\otimes t)\approx s

By associativity of
\otimes
, we can rewrite the claim as

({r_{1}}\otimes r_{2})\otimes t\approx s.

For compatibility of
\approx
and
\otimes
(
Lemma

19
),
we can use (H1C) to simplify the claim even more to:

r\otimes t\approx s.

and conclude with (H3).
∎

This result justifies the notation
\bigodot_{i=0}^{k}{(\phi_{i})^{\Delta_{i}}}
as a shorthand for

{({({(\phi_{0})^{\Delta_{0}}}\odot{(\phi_{1})^{\Delta_{1}}})^{\Delta_{0}\Join\Delta_{1}}}\ldots\odot{(\phi_{k})^{\Delta_{k}}})^{\Delta_{0}\Join\Delta_{1}\Join\ldots\Join\Delta_{k}}}

with
\odot\in\{*,\land\}
.
Thanks to this result,
we can also give a Hilbert-style proof system for CSL,
based on the standard one for BI from
[
18
]
;
see
Figure

3
.

Lemma 27

(Soundness of
\cdot\vdash^{\Delta}\cdot
)
.

For every pair of formulas
{(\phi)^{\Delta}},{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\in\Phi\Delta
,
we have that:

\left({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\vdash^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left({(\phi)^{\Delta}}\models^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right).

Proof.

By induction on the proof relation. In particular,
\land_{i}
is a consequence of

Lemma

23
. Commutativity and associativity of
*
follow from
Lemma

26
.
∎

4.4
CSL and Other Separation Logics

4.4.1
CSL vs. BI

From a syntactic point of view,
CSL is the conjunctive fragment of BI where
every formula
\phi
is tagged with an environment
\Delta

such that
\Delta\vdash\phi
. In contrast with
what happens with BI, by employing this syntax, we
do not need to use the notion of
footprint
for programs and
formulas
[
17
,
26
]
to denote the minimal set of variables that
these objects depends on, because this set is now explicit
in the formula. This choice comes with almost no additional cost,
as environments are anyway needed to enforce the polytime constraint on our language.

From a semantic point of view, we model CSL on
stores, i.e. pairs of an
effectively samplable
distribution ensemble
and an environment. These objects,
in turn, form a
partial Kripke resource monoid
with the pre-order

\approx\sqsubseteq
and the tensor product as composition.

Proposition 1
.

The structure

(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\otimes},\mathbf{0},\approx\sqsubseteq)
is a partial
Kripke resource monoid.
∎

Proof.

The associativity of
\otimes
is a consequence of the associativity
of environments’ union and of multiplication.
Now we show that
\mathbf{0}
is the identity for
\otimes
:

\displaystyle(\{\{\varepsilon^{1}\}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}^{\varepsilon}\otimes{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})_{n}(m)

\displaystyle={((\{\varepsilon^{1}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{R}_{n})(m))^{\varepsilon\Join\Delta}}

\displaystyle={(\{\varepsilon^{1}\}(m_{\emptyset})\cdot\mathcal{R}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}))^{\Delta}}

\displaystyle={(1\cdot\mathcal{R}_{n}(m_{\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)}))^{\Delta}}

\displaystyle={(\mathcal{R}_{n}(m))^{\Delta}}

The proof of the right identity of
\mathbf{0}
is analogous. Now, we show that
\approx\sqsubseteq
is a pre-order. Reflexivity is trivial since both the relations are reflexive. Transitivity can be shown as follows:

\displaystyle s\approx r\sqsubseteq t\approx u\sqsubseteq v

\displaystyle\models\big(\exists t^{\prime}.s\approx r\approx t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq u\sqsubseteq v\big)

\displaystyle\models\big(\exists t^{\prime}.s\approx t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq v\big)\vdash s\approx\sqsubseteq v

Where the second step is a consequence of Lemma
20
, and the third is a consequence of the transitivity of the two relations
\approx,\sqsubseteq
. It remains us to show that
\approx\sqsubseteq
is compatible with
\otimes
.
In particular, this requires to show that

\forall s,r,t.s\approx\sqsubseteq r\Rightarrow(s\otimes t)\downarrow\Rightarrow(r\otimes t)\downarrow\Rightarrow(s\otimes t)\approx\sqsubseteq(r\otimes t)

and

\forall s,r,t.s\approx\sqsubseteq r\Rightarrow(t\otimes s)\downarrow\Rightarrow(t\otimes r)\downarrow\Rightarrow(t\otimes s)\approx\sqsubseteq(t\otimes r)

We show only the first claim, the proof of the second is analogous. Assume that
s\approx\sqsubseteq r
,
(s\otimes t)
is defined, and that
(r\otimes t)
is defined. From
s\approx\sqsubseteq r
we deduce that there is a state
u
such that
s\approx u\sqsubseteq r
. Let
t\in\mathcal{S}
be a store. From Lemma
19
, we obtain
s\otimes t\approx u\otimes t
and from Lemma
22
, we obtain
u\otimes t\sqsubseteq r\otimes t
. This concludes the proof of the compatibility of
\approx\sqsubseteq
and
\otimes
.
∎

In order to ease the comparison between these two logics, in
the following we call
\Phi_{BI}
the set of conjunctive BI’s formulas
and, given a formula
\phi\in\Phi_{BI}
, we write
\phi=\left[{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right]

if
\phi
can be obtained by removing all the environment notations from
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.
Thanks to
proposition

1
, we can model
\Phi_{BI}
on
partial Kripke resource monoids
.
This will allow us, to stipulate a semantic correspondence between these two logics.
The semantics of
\Phi_{BI}
is in
Figure

4
. Notice that we use
\Vdash
instead
of
\models
, as we do for CSL’s formulas.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathbf{U}(e)}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\mathcal{S}}\mid

\displaystyle\;\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\approx\mathsf{unif}(\tau)\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathbf{CI}(e,g)}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\mathcal{S}}\mid

\displaystyle\;\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\approx\llbracket\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathbf{EQ}(e,g)}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\mathcal{S}}\mid

\displaystyle\;\llbracket\Delta\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})=\llbracket\Delta\vdash g:\tau\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\mathbf{IS}(d,c)}\rrbracket\mathrel{:=}\{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\mathcal{S}}\mid

\displaystyle\;\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\forall m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n}).\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash d:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis\Delta\vdash c:\tau\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)\}

{s}\Vdash{A}

iff
s\in\llbracket A\rrbracket

{s}\Vdash{\top}

always

{s}\Vdash{\bot}

never

{s}\Vdash\phi\land\psi

iff
{s}\Vdash\phi
and
{s}\Vdash\psi

{s}\Vdash{\phi*\psi}

iff there are
r,t
such that
r\otimes t\downarrow
,
r\otimes t\approx\sqsubseteq s
,
{r}\Vdash{{\phi}}
and

{t}\Vdash{{\psi}}

Figure 4:
Semantics of BI

The semantics in
Figure

4
is very close to CSL’s one, with the exception that
we do not impose any restriction on the domain of the stores. For this reason, the semantics
of
\Phi_{BI}
formulas is less strict that the one of CSL’s formula.
This is why from the validity of a CSL’s formula

{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
in a store
s
,
we can deduce the validity of the structurally identical
formula (but without environment annotations) in the same store,
using the BI interpretation.

Proposition 2
.

For every environment
\Delta
, CSL formula

{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}
, and
store
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
, we have:

s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\Rightarrow s\Vdash\left[{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right],

where
\left[{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right]
is the BI formula that is obtained by removing
the environment tags from
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.
∎

Proof.

Direct consequence of
Lemma

28
below.
∎

Lemma 28
.

\forall\phi\in\Phi_{BI}.\forall\Delta.\forall{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}.\phi=\left[{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right]\Rightarrow\forall s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\Rightarrow s\Vdash\phi.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on
\phi
, we treat the case of atomic propositions uniformly:

-

(
A
). Let
\Delta
be an environment that types all the expressions in
A
(with a unique type if there are multiple expressions). Fix
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
, and assume
s\models^{\Delta}{(A)^{\Delta}}
, which is
s\in\llbracket{(A)^{\Delta}}\rrbracket
,
observe that for every environment
\Delta
, we have
\llbracket{(A)^{\Delta}}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket
. This observation shows the claim.

-

(
\top,\bot
). Trivial.

-

(
\phi=\psi\land\xi
). Fix an environment
\Delta
, and assume that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
is structurally identical to
\phi
and that it belongs to
{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}
. This means that there are two CSL formulas
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Xi}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Theta}}
(with
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta
and
\Theta\sqsubseteq\Delta
) such that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}={({(\psi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}
and notably:

\psi=\left[{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right]\quad\xi=\left[{(\xi)^{\Theta}}\right].

Now, we fix
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
, and we assume that

s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}},

which means that there are
r\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
and
t\in\llbracket\Theta\rrbracket

such that
r\sqsubseteq s
and
t\sqsubseteq s
, and notably:

r\models^{\Xi}{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\quad t\models^{\Theta}{(\xi)^{\Theta}}

Let
{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Delta}}
be the formulas that are obtained by exchanging the most external environments of the formulas
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
(i.e.
\Xi
and
\Theta
) with
\Delta
. Observe that
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}\sqsubseteq{(\xi)^{\Delta}}
. For this reason, we can apply
Lemma

23
to (HA) and (HB), to show that

s\models^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\quad s\models^{\Delta}{(\xi)^{\Delta}}

From (H1) and (H2), we also deduce:

\psi=\left[{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right]\quad\xi=\left[{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\right].

The claim is a consequence of the IHs on
\phi
and
\psi
and of the hypotheses (H1’), (HA’), (H2’), (HB’).

-

(
\phi=\psi*\xi
). Fix an environment
\Delta
, and assume that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
is structurally identical to
\phi
and belongs to
{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}
. This means that there are two CSL formulas
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Xi}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Theta}}
(with
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta
and
\Theta\sqsubseteq\Delta
) such that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}={({(\psi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}
and notably:

\psi=\left[{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right]\quad\xi=\left[{(\xi)^{\Theta}}\right].

Now, we fix
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
, and we assume that

s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}},

which means that there are
r\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
and
t\in\llbracket\Theta\rrbracket

such that
r\approx\sqsubseteq s
and
t\approx\sqsubseteq s
, and notably:

r\models^{\Xi}{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\quad t\models^{\Theta}{(\xi)^{\Theta}},

and finally:

r\mathrel{\otimes}t\approx\sqsubseteq s.

By applying the IHs on
\phi
and
\psi
and using the hypotheses (H1), (HA), (H2), (HB),
we obtain that

r\Vdash\psi\quad t\Vdash\xi,

Then, we finally observe that this is sufficient to establish

s\Vdash\psi*\xi

because of (HC).

∎

For the converse direction, we need to be more careful:
it is not true that if a conjunctive BI formula
\phi
is valid in a state
s
,
then the same formula is valid in the same state using the CSL semantics

independently
of the environments that we use to tag that formula.

Example 4
.

Let
\Delta={r,s,t:\mathsf{Bool}}
and

{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
be the store that
where
r
and
s
are uniform and independent, and where
for every value of the security parameter
n
,
and for every
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})

m(t)=m(s)
. Observe that
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}
satisfies

\mathbf{CI}(r,r)*\mathbf{CI}(s,s)
according to the BI semantics,
but it does not satisfy every formula
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
that is
obtained by labeling the subformulas of
\phi
with environments.
In particular, this does not hold for the formula:

{({(\mathbf{CI}(r,r))^{r,t:\mathsf{Bool}}}*{(\mathbf{CI}(s,s))^{s:\mathsf{Bool}}})^{\Delta}}

because
s
and
t
are not computationally independent.
∎

This shows that, given the BI-validity of a conjunctive formula
\phi

in a state
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
, the CSL-validity
of a structurally identical formula
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
on the same state,
depends on the underlying sets of independent variables.
This happens because, due to the annotation of formulas with environments,
the semantics of the separating conjunction in CSL become less
ambiguous than in BI, and the translation from BI to CSL requires this ambiguity to be resolved. However, notice that since we work with
conjunctive operators only, the tags of the two subformulas
of a separating conjunction can be syntactically
under-approximated

by taking the restriction of the global environment to the set of variables
that appear in these formulas.

Proposition 3
.

For every environment
\Delta
, store
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
and conjunctive BI formula
\phi
, if

s\Vdash\phi,

there is a formula
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
of
CSL that is structurally identical to
\phi
such that:

s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}.

∎

Proof.

The claim that we want to show is:

\forall\phi\in\Phi_{BI}.\forall\Delta,\forall s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.s\Vdash\phi\Rightarrow\exists{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\in{(\Phi)^{\Delta}}.\phi=\left[{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right]\land s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}

-

(
A
). Trivial.

-

(
\top,\bot
). Trivial.

-

(
\phi=\psi\land\xi
). We fix
\Delta
and
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
and from the definition of the semantics, we observe:

s\Vdash\psi\quad s\Vdash\xi.

Then, we apply the two IHs on the two subformulas,
s
and
\Delta
, this shows the existence of two formulas
{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Delta}}
such that
\psi=\left[{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right]
,
\xi=\left[{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\right]
, and notably:

s\models^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\quad s\models^{\Delta}{(\xi)^{\Delta}}.

Since
s\sqsubseteq s
, we can conclude that

s\models^{\Delta}{({(\psi)^{\Delta}}\land{(\xi)^{\Delta}})^{\Delta}}.

Finally, observe that
\psi\land\xi\left[{({(\psi)^{\Delta}}\land{(\xi)^{\Delta}})^{\Delta}}\right]
.

-

(
\phi=\psi*\xi
). we fix
\Delta
and
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
,
and from the definition of the semantics, we observe that there are
r,t\in\mathcal{S}
such that

r\mathrel{\otimes}t\approx\sqsubseteq s\quad r\Vdash\psi\quad r\Vdash\xi.

From (H1), we can also conclude that
r\in\llbracket\Xi\rrbracket
for some
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta

and that
t\in\llbracket\Theta\rrbracket
for some
\Theta\sqsubseteq\Delta
.
Then, we apply the two IHs on the two subformulas,
r,t
and
\Xi,\Theta
, using the hypotheses (H1) and (H2). This shows the existence of two formulas
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
and
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
such that
\psi=\left[{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right]
,
\xi=\left[{(\xi)^{\Theta}}\right]
, and notably:

r\models^{\Xi}{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\quad t\models^{\Theta}{(\xi)^{\Theta}}.

From (H1), we can conclude that:

s\models^{\Delta}{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Theta}}.

Finally, observe that
\psi*\xi=\left[{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right]
.

∎

4.4.2
CSL vs. PSL

A comparison between CSL and Barthe et al.’s PSL is now in order.
First of all, the semantics of the separating conjunction is more restrictive
than that in PSL: in addition to knowing that the substates which satisfy the
sub-formulas are not statistically but
computationally
independent, we
also know the domains of their environments.
For instance, if:

s\models^{\Delta}{({(\top)^{m:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}})^{\Delta}},

(1)

we know
that there are two computationally independent substates of
s
,
namely
r,t
,
such that
r\models{(\top)^{m:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}
,

t\models{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}
.
In addition to this, we also know that
r
and
t
store
only
m
and
k
, respectively.

Also observe that in CSL it is possible to state the
computational independence of (sets of) variables naturally:
with the condition
s\models{(\top)^{m:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*{{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}}
, we are
stating that the projection of
s
on
m
is computationally independent from
its projection on
k
, without imposing further assumptions on the value of
m
.
This semantics can be achieved in
PSL by replacing
{(\top)^{m:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}
with a
tautological atomic proposition
A
such that
\mathsf{FV}(A)=\{m\}
.

In contrast to what happens for PSL, CSL’s equations are always reflexive.
This is not always the case for PSL: for instance,

\mathbf{CI}(e,e)
is not valid,
as it does not hold, for instance, in the empty store.

5
Inference Rules

\vdash\left\{\phi\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{skip}\;\left\{\phi\right\}\qquad\vdash\left\{\phi\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}\;\left\{\xi\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{\phi\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}\vdash\left\{\psi\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}\;\left\{\xi\right\}\qquad

\vdash\left\{\top\right\}\;\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\;\left\{\mathbf{EQ}(r,e)\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow er\not\in\mathsf{FV}(e)\qquad\vdash\left\{\top\right\}\;\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow d\;\left\{\mathbf{IS}(r,d)\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow dr\not\in\mathsf{FV}(d)

\left\{{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\;\left\{{({({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\mathbf{EQ}(r,e))^{\Xi\cup\{r:\tau\}}})^{\Xi\cup\{r:\tau\}}}*{(\psi)^{\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\Xi\vdash e:\tau r\notin\mathsf{FV}(e){(\psi)^{\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}}\in\Phi r\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)

\left\{{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\psi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow d\;\left\{{({({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\mathbf{IS}(r,d))^{\Xi\cup\{r:\tau\}}})^{\Xi\cup\{r:\tau\}}}*{(\psi)^{\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\Xi\vdash d:\tau r\notin\mathsf{FV}(d){(\psi)^{\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}}\in\Phi r\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Xi)

\vdash\left\{\top\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\;\left\{\psi\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\vdash\left\{\mathbf{IS}(r,1)\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}\vdash\left\{\mathbf{IS}(r,0)\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}\;\left\{\psi\right\}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\in{\Phi_{\mathsf{ex}}}

Figure 5:
Computational Rules

In this section, we show how CSL can be turned into a Hoare logic for
reasoning about computational independence and pseudorandomness. We first
define Hoare triples, then we provide a set of rules for reasoning about
probabilistic programs. Finally, we prove the soundness of our system, and we
show how it can be used to reason about the security of some classic
cryptographic primitives: in
Section

6.1
we use this system of rules
to show the computational secrecy of
a variation of the OTP, where the key
is not
drawn from a

statistically
uniform distribution, but rather from a
pseudorandom
one.
In
Section

6.3
, we show that our rule for
\mathtt{if}

statements can be used to show the correctness of a program that computes
the exclusive or.
Finally, in
Section

6.3
we use this logic to show the security of
the standard construction in which a pseudorandom generator with low expansion
factor is composed with itself to obtain another pseudorandom generator
with higher expansion factor.

5.1
Hoare Triples

A
Hoare triple
is an expression in the form

\left\{{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right\}

(2)

For the sake of simplicity, we write
\left\{\phi\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}

or
\left\{\phi\right\}\;\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}

instead of
(
2
) when this does not create any ambiguity. The Hoare triple
(
2
) is said to be
valid
if

\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}
and for every store
\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
such
that
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
, it holds that

\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}})\models{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
. In that case,
we write

\models\left\{\phi\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}.

Fortunately, Hoare triples do not need to be proved valid by hand, and we can
define a certain number of inference rules, which can then be proved to be
validity-preserving. In the following subsection, we are going to introduce two
categories of inference rules, namely computational and structural rules. Each
rule in the former class matches a specific instruction and does not require
any other triple as a premise. As such, computational rules are meant to
draw conclusions about how the state evolves when the corresponding instruction
is executed. On the other hand, structural rules are used to obtain
a new triple about a given program
\mathtt{P}
from another one about
\mathtt{P}

itself, this way altering preconditions and postconditions.

\displaystyle\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{skip})

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathsf{MV}(r\leftarrow e)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\{r\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{P})\cup\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{if}\;e\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{P})\cup\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{R})

Figure 6:
Definition of
\mathsf{MV}(\cdot)
.

\vdash\left\{{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\eta)^{\Delta}}\right\}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\models{(\xi)^{\Delta}}{(\eta)^{\Delta}}\models{(\psi)^{\Delta}}

\vdash\left\{{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\wedge{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}\wedge{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)\cap\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{P})=\emptyset

\vdash\left\{{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\vdash\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}

Figure 7:
Structural Rules

5.2
Computational Rules

CSL’s computational rules can be found in Figure
5
.
The rules
\mathsf{Skip}
and
\mathsf{Seq}
are self explanatory, while the rules for
assignments and conditionals require some more discussion.
First of all, we note the presence of four different rules for assignments. two
of them, namely
\mathsf{Assn}
and
\mathsf{SRAssn}
are concerned with arbitrary
expressions and the
\mathbf{EQ}(\cdot,\cdot)
predicate. The other two, namely

\mathsf{DAssn}
and
\mathsf{SDAssn}
, are instead concerned with deterministic expressions
and the
\mathbf{IS}(\cdot,\cdot)
predicate.

\mathsf{Assn}
is arguably the simplest of the four rules, and is present in Barthe
et al.’s PSL. The
\mathsf{DAssn}
rule, instead can be applied only to deterministic
assignments and yields a very precise conclusion: a state
\mathcal{R}
satisfies
the conclusion
\mathbf{IS}(r,d)

if and only if, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every sample
m

of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, the semantics of the deterministic
expressions
d
evaluated on
m
is equal to the
deterministic semantics of
r
evaluated on
m
.
In particular, this rule is useful for indistinguishability proofs.
An example of fruitful use of this rule can be found in

Section

6.1
below.

The
separating assignment rules

\mathsf{SRAssn}
and
\mathsf{SDAssn}
are used
to produce triples with separating conjunctions in the pre- and post-conditions.
Intuitively, these rules are meant to describe how independence propagates
through assignments: if there are two independent
portions of a store, and an expression that can be
evaluated in one of these is assigned to a variable
r
, then

r
is independent from the other portion of the store.
In addition, the assignment does not invalidate any other
formula
{(\psi)^{\Theta}}
that is already valid in that portion of
the store, provided that
\psi
does not mention
r
.

Finally, notice that the standard assignment rules
are not instances of the separating ones. For example,
if we show
{({(\mathbf{EQ}(e,g))^{\Xi}}*{(\phi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}
,
We cannot deduce
{(\mathbf{EQ}(e,g))^{\Delta}}
from it, because
Lemma

24

holds only for approximate rules.

Let us now switch our attention to the rule
\mathsf{RCond}
. This rule has a very
specific formulation. This is due to the peculiar semantic underlying CSL,
which is based on
efficiently samplable
distribution ensembles.
In particular, as far as the authors of this
paper know, it is not known whether efficiently samplable
distribution ensembles are closed under conditioning.

For this reason, when showing the soundness of this rule, we cannot
condition the initial state on the value of the guard,
as it happens in
[
17
]
. As a consequence, it is challenging
to state this rule with a more general pre-condition.

In
Section

6
we show how this rule, although
having a limited expressive power, can nevertheless be employed on a small
example dealing with the exclusive or.

Proposition 4
.

The computational rules are sound.
∎

Proof.

We do not show the validity of the typing judgment associated to a triple, because it is trivial. For the logical soundness, we want to show that every triple
\left\{{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right\}

that we can deduce using the computational rules is sound, namely:
