\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{.}\left({(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})\models{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}}\right)

We go by induction on the proof of
\vdash\left\{{{(\phi)^{\Delta}}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}}\right\}
:

-

(
\mathsf{Skip}
). The claim is

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{.}\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{skip}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{{(\phi)^{\Delta}}}\right).

This holds because
\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{skip}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})={{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}}
.

-

(
\mathsf{Seq}
). We are asked to show:

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}}\right)

under the rule’s preconditions.
Since we know that the rule
\mathsf{Seq}
was applied, we also know that
there is
{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}}
such that
\vdash\left\{{{(\phi)^{\Delta}}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}}\right\}
and
\vdash\left\{{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}\;\left\{{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}}\right\}
,
the two IHs on these sub-derivation state:

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}}\right)

and

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}}\right)

Fix
{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}}
and assume
\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right)
, the goal of the sub-derivation becomes:

\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P};\mathtt{R}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}}

which is

\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\right)\models{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}}.

From the first IH we obtain
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}}
, using this hypothesis as premise of the second IH, we get the claim.

-

(
\mathsf{Assn}
).
In this sub-derivation, the goal is to show:

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models\top\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models\mathbf{EQ}(r,e)\right)

under the rule’s preconditions (
r\notin\mathsf{FV}(e)
).
This claim can be restated as:

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{\mathbf{EQ}(r,e)}\right)

From now on, due to the lack of ambiguity we are going to omit the judging statement from the notation of the triples. The claim can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\llbracket r\rrbracket\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right)=\llbracket e\rrbracket\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right).

We will pass through the following equivalence and concluding for transitivity:

\displaystyle\llbracket r\rrbracket\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right)=\llbracket e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})=\llbracket e\rrbracket\left(\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})\right).

Which is the claim of
Lemma

11
.

-

(
\mathsf{RCond}
). We want to show that
\models\left\{\top\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}
, which is equivalent to:

\begin{multlined}\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})\models{(\psi)^{\Delta}},\end{multlined}

with the following assumptions:

-

(H1).
\vdash\left\{\mathbf{IS}(r,1)\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}
,

-

(H2).
\vdash\left\{\mathbf{IS}(r,0)\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{R}\;\left\{\psi\right\}
,

-

(H3).
{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\in{\Phi_{\mathsf{ex}}}
.

We assume that
{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models\top
.
Assume that for some
b
:

\sum_{{m:m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})\land m(r)=b}}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{n}(m)=0,

In this case, it is possible to show that the
semantics of the statement is equal to
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(s)

or to
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket(s)
and to conclude with one of the IHs.
In the remaining part of the proof, we can
assume without loss of generality that
(*)
does not hold for any
b\in\mathbb{B}
.
In this case, we expand the semantics of the statement obtaining that,
for some fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
it is equal to:

{\text{bind}\left(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}({\text{unit}(m)})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}\right)},

for every
m
and
b
,
let
\mathcal{Q}^{n^{\prime},m,b}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
be a distribution ensemble
such that
\mathcal{Q}^{n^{\prime},m,b}_{n}(m)=1
if
n^{\prime}=n
,
and that for every
n\neq n^{\prime}
,
and every
\overline{m}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{Q}^{n^{\prime}m,b}_{n})

\llparenthesis r\rrparenthesis_{\overline{n}^{\prime}}(\overline{m})=b
.
Notice that every
\mathcal{Q}^{n^{\prime},m,b}
is a family of efficiently samplable
distributions such that
{(\mathcal{Q}^{n^{\prime},m,b})^{\Delta}}\models{(\mathbf{IS}(r,b))^{\Delta}}
if

m(r)=b
.
The semantics
of the conditional can
be restated as follows:

{\text{bind}\left(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\begin{cases}\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1}_{n})&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,0}_{n})&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}\right)},

which in turn is equal to:

{\text{bind}\left(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\begin{cases}\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}\right)_{n}&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\left(\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,0})}\right)_{n}&\text{if }m(r)=0\end{cases}\right)}.

We can assume without loss of generality that
\psi

is a conjunction of
{(A({e_{i}},{g_{i}}))^{\Xi_{i}}}
for
0\leq i\leq k
,
where
A
can either be
\mathbf{EQ}(\cdot,\cdot)
or
\mathbf{IS}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
Indeed, we know that
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
is a conjunction of smaller formulas
by definition of exact formulas.
If any of these smaller formulas or propositions is
{(\bot)^{\Xi_{j}}}
,
we conclude ad absurdum, because no state satisfies such formula,
conversely, if any of these smaller formulas is
{(\top)^{\Xi_{j}}}
,
we can show that the final state satisfies the formula
{(\psi^{\prime})^{\Delta}}

obtained removing this formula from the conjunction, which is equivalent to
\psi
.
No other cases are possible.
If
{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
has this shape, our claim is that there are two finite set of triples

–

E=\{({(e_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},{(g_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},\tau_{i})\mid 0\leq i\leq k\}

–

S=\{({(d_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},{(c_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},\tau_{i})\mid 0\leq i\leq l\}

such that:

–

for every triple
({(e_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},{(g_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},\tau_{i})\in E
,
it holds that

\llbracket\Xi_{i}\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket}(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}})=\llbracket\Xi_{i}\vdash g_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket}(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}}).

–

for every triple
({(d_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},{(c_{i})^{\Xi_{i}}},\tau_{i})\in S
,
every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

m\in{\mathsf{supp}}({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}}),

we have:

\llparenthesis\Xi_{i}\vdash d_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis\Xi_{i}\vdash c_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrparenthesis_{n}(m).

We start by showing the first conclusion.
For
Lemma

5
, we can show an equivalent result:

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket}(\mathcal{R}))=\\
\llbracket\Delta\vdash g_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket}(\mathcal{R}))

We fix some
i
, and we show that this result holds for
e_{i}
,
g_{i}
.
This means that for every
n
, we require:

\llbracket\Delta\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket_{n}({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}}(\mathcal{R}))=\llbracket\Delta\vdash g_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket_{n}({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket_{n}}(\mathcal{R}))

(C)

Fix
n
, it holds that the term on the left is equivalent to

\text{bind}(\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},h),\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}})=\text{bind}(\mathcal{R}_{n},m\mapsto\text{bind}(h(m),\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}})).

where
h(m)
is

\begin{cases}\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}\right)_{n}&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\left(\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,0})}\right)_{n}&\text{if }m(r)=0.\end{cases}

It is possible to express in these terms also the expression on the right of (C).
For this reason, it suffices to conclude the proof by showing that for every

m\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
:

\text{bind}(h(m),\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}})=\text{bind}(h(m),\llbracket g_{i}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}).

Thus, we fix
m
, and we assume without loss of generality
that
m(r)=1
. The claim reduces to:

\text{bind}(\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}\right)_{n},\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}})=\text{bind}(\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}\right)_{n},\llbracket g_{i}\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}),

which is:

\llbracket e_{i}\rrbracket_{n}({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}_{n})=\llbracket g_{i}\rrbracket_{n}({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}_{n}).

For the IH, we conclude that
{\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}\models^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
. So, in particular, we have that:

\llbracket\Xi_{i}\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}})=\llbracket\Xi_{i}\vdash g_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}}).

By applying
Lemma

5
,
we obtain:

\llbracket{e_{i}}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}})=\llbracket{g_{i}}\rrbracket({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}),

in particular:

\llbracket{e_{i}}\rrbracket_{n}({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}_{n})=\llbracket{g_{i}}\rrbracket_{n}({\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}_{n}),

which is our claim.
Now we go to the second result; in particular we fix some
i,n
, and we show a stronger result: for every
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket}(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}})_{n})
, we have that
\llparenthesis\Xi_{i}\vdash d_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)=\llparenthesis\Xi_{i}\vdash c_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrparenthesis_{n}(m)
.
From
m\in{\mathsf{supp}}(({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket}(\mathcal{R})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}})_{n})
, and
Remark

5
, we deduce that there is
m^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(({\llbracket\mathtt{if}\;r\;\mathtt{then}\;\mathtt{P}\;\mathtt{else}\;\mathtt{R}\rrbracket}(\mathcal{R}))_{n})
such that
m^{\prime}_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi_{i})}=m
.
From
Lemma

13
, we deduce that there is
\overline{m}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
such that
m^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\text{bind}(h(\overline{m}),w\mapsto w_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi_{i})}))
where
h(m)
is

\begin{cases}\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}\right)_{n}&\text{if }m(r)=1\\
\left(\llbracket\mathtt{R}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,0})}\right)_{n}&\text{if }m(r)=0.\end{cases}

We assume without lack of generality that
\overline{m}(r)=1
. In particular, this means that
m^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\text{bind}(\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}\right)_{n},w\to w_{\mathsf{dom}(\Xi_{i})}))={\mathsf{supp}}({((\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}})_{n}})
. For IH, we conclude that
{\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})}}\models^{\Delta}{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
. So, in particular, we know that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
w\in{\mathsf{supp}}(((\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{(\mathcal{Q}^{n,m,1})})_{\Delta\to\Xi_{i}})_{n})
, we have that
\llparenthesis\Xi_{i}\vdash d_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrparenthesis_{n}(w)=\llparenthesis\Xi_{i}\vdash c_{i}:\tau_{i}\rrparenthesis_{n}(w)
. In particular, this holds for
m^{\prime}
, but it also holds for
m
because of
Lemma

3
.

-

(
\mathsf{DAssn}
).
We start by assuming fixing
\Delta
, a state
s=\mathcal{R}^{\Delta}
, and by assuming that
\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow d
and that
r\notin\mathsf{FV}(d)
. Observe that
s\models^{\Delta}\top
. For this reason, the goal is to show that we have
s\models^{\Delta}\mathbf{IS}(r,d)
, which is equivalent to showing that for every
l\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\llbracket r\leftarrow d\rrbracket(s))
, we have
\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\llparenthesis r\rrparenthesis_{n}(l)=\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(l)
. This is exactly the claim of
Lemma

14
.

-

(
\mathsf{SRAssn}
). We start by assuming that there is a state
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
that satisfies the premise, this means that there are
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
such that:

-

(H1).
s_{1}\models^{\Xi}{(\phi)^{\Xi}}
.

-

(H2).
s_{2}\models^{\Theta}{(\psi)^{\Theta}}
.

-

(H3).
s_{1}\otimes{s_{2}}\approx\sqsubseteq s
.

The goal is to show that there are
r_{1}\in\llbracket\Xi\cup\{r:\tau\}\rrbracket
,
r_{2}\in\llbracket\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}\rrbracket

such that

-

(C1).
r_{1}\models^{\Xi\cup\{r:\tau\}}{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\mathbf{EQ}(r,e))^{\Xi\cup\{r:\tau\}}}
.

-

(C2).
r_{2}\models^{\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}{(\psi)^{\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}}
.

-

(C3).
r_{1}\otimes{r_{2}}\approx\sqsubseteq\llbracket r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s)
.

We define
\Omega
as
\{r:\tau\}

and we call
u\in\llbracket\Omega\rrbracket
the store that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
is the Dirac distribution on a memory
\overline{m}_{n}\in\llbracket\Omega\rrbracket_{n}^{\mathtt{d}}
.
The store
r_{1}
, is
\llbracket\Xi\Join\Omega\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{1}\otimes u)
, and the store
r_{2}
is
{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}
.
From
Lemma

8
, we deduce that
\llbracket\Xi\Join\Omega\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{1}\otimes u)_{\Xi\Join\Omega\to\Xi}={(s_{1}\otimes u)}_{\Xi\Join\Omega\to\Xi}=s_{1}
. This shows that
(r_{1})_{\Xi\Join\Omega\to\Xi}\models{(\phi)^{\Xi}}
; in order to show the claim (C1), it remains to show that
r_{1}\models\mathbf{EQ}(r,e)
holds as well, but this is a consequence of
Lemma

11
.
The proof of (C2) relies on
Lemma

23
and (H2). Then we go to (C3): by definition, we have that
r_{1}\otimes r_{2}=\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{1}\otimes u)\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}
from
Lemma

9
, we conclude that:

\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{1}\otimes u)\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}=\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{1}\otimes u\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}})
. From compatibility of
\sqsubseteq
with
\otimes

Lemma

22
, and from reflexivity of
\sqsubseteq
, we deduce that

s_{1}\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\approx\sqsubseteq s_{1}\otimes s_{2}
, so from (H3), transitivity of
\approx\sqsubseteq
and this observation, we conclude
s_{1}\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\approx\sqsubseteq s
. From the definition of
\approx\sqsubseteq
, we deduce that
s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\approx s_{1}\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}
. From this observation and compatibility of
\approx
with
\otimes
,
Lemma

19
, we deduce that:
s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\otimes u\approx s_{1}\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\otimes u
, and we conclude with
Lemma

10
, by showing that:
\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{1}\otimes{s_{2}}_{\Theta\to\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\otimes u)\approx\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\otimes u)
. From
Lemma

15
, we also deduce that:
\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\}}\otimes u)=\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta\Join\Omega})
. From
Lemma

6
, we conclude
\llbracket\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to{\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})}})\sqsubseteq\llbracket\Delta\vdash r\leftarrow e\rrbracket(s)_{\Delta\to{\Xi\cup\Omega\Join(\Theta\setminus\{r:\tau\})}}
. This shows (C3), and concludes the soundness proof for the first rule.

-

(
\mathsf{SDAssn}
). The soundness proof of this rule is completely analogous to the one we have just shown, the main difference is that (C1) would require to show that
\Xi\models^{r_{1}}\mathbf{IS}(e,d)
instead of
\Xi\models^{r_{1}}\mathbf{CI}(e,e)
. This requires the application of
Lemma

14
instead of
Lemma

11
. The rest of the proof is identical.

∎

5.3
Structural Rules

Structural rules are defined in Figure
7

and are roughly inspired from those from
[
17
]
, at the same time being
significantly simpler.

The rule
\mathsf{Weak}
is standard, while the other two rules are more interesting.

\mathsf{Const}
is based on the ordinary rule
for constancy of Hoare logic: it states that is always possible to add
any formula
\xi
as a conjunct of the precondition
and the postcondition if this formula does not mention
variables which are modified by the program. The set of modified variables can be over approximated
by the function
\mathsf{MV}(\cdot)
defined in Figure
6
.
The intuition behind the soundness of this rule is that
the projection of the initial state where
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}

is evaluated is not affected by the execution of
\mathtt{P}
.

The
\mathsf{Frame}
rule is somehow similar to the
\mathsf{Const}
rule,
but introduces a separating conjunction: by means of this rule, it is possible
to show that some operations
preserve computational independence.
The rule is sound because, by the definition of formulas,
none of the variables where
{(\xi)^{\Theta}}
is interpreted
appears in
\Xi
, because
\Xi\Join\Theta
is
implicitly proved to be well-defined. Moreover, from the first premise of the
rule, we deduce that

\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}
, so the variables in the domain of

\Theta
are not influenced by the execution of

\mathtt{P}
. This is a sufficient condition to ensure that
computational indistinguishability is preserved during the
execution of
\mathtt{P}
.

Notice that the way in which the store is split
is induced by the annotation in the formula.
This choice results in a less general
rule compared to that of PSL
[
17
]
because,
in particular,
\phi
and
\psi
are interpreted
in the same environment
\Xi
, which means that this
rule cannot be applied in all situations in which
\mathtt{P}
modifies the
statistical dependencies between the variables.
This explains the need for the two
non-derivable
separating assignment rules

\mathsf{SRAssn}
and
\mathsf{SDAssn}
,
which allow the movement of variables from one side of the
separating conjunction to the other.

Even though more fine-grained formulations
of the
\mathsf{Frame}
rule are valid,
we decided to adopt this one given the good trade-off between
simplicity and expressiveness it provides.

Proposition 5
.

The structural rules are sound.
∎

Proof.

As we did for
Proposition

4
, we do not show
\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}
; this means that we will only show the logical correctness of this system. To do so, we go by induction on the proof of the triple.

-

(
\mathsf{Weak}
). In this case, we are asked to show that
\models\left\{\phi\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{\psi\right\}
knowing that:

-

(H1).
\vdash\left\{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\eta)^{\Delta}}\right\}
,

-

(H2).
{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\models{(\xi)^{\Delta}}
,

-

(H3).
{(\eta)^{\Delta}}\models{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
.

Applying the IH to H1, we obtain
\models\left\{{(\xi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\eta)^{\Delta}}\right\}
(H4). The claim is:

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models\phi\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models\xi\right)

Fix
\mathcal{R}
, from (H2) we obtain that
{{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}}
satisfies
{(\xi)^{\Delta}}
as well. Applying (H4), we obtain
\left(\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{(\eta)^{\Delta}}\right)
, so we conclude with (H3).

-

(
\mathsf{Const}
). We want to show that
\models\left\{{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}
under the assumptions:

-

(H1).
{\vdash\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}}
,

-

(H2).
\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)\cap\mathsf{MV}(\mathtt{P})=\emptyset
.

Applying the IH to (H1), we conclude
\models\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}
(H3). The claim is:

\displaystyle\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right)

Fix
\mathcal{R}
and assume
{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}\land{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}
. From this assumption, and the definition of
\sqsubseteq
, we deduce that:

{{(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})^{\Xi}}}\models{(\phi)^{\Xi}}

and that

{{(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Theta})^{\Theta}}}\models{(\xi)^{\Theta}}

simply by inlining the definition of
\cdot\models\cdot
.
From (H3), we conclude
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})^{\Xi}}})\models{(\phi)^{\Xi}}
(C2). Observe that this object is defined because for validity of the premise (H3), we
have
\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}
.
Lemma

6
shows that

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({(\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\to\Xi})^{\Xi}})\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}).

From this observation. Finally, with an application of Lemma
8
, we observe that:

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})_{\Delta\to\Theta}={{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}_{\Delta\to\Theta}}{}.

The conclusion is given by (C1-4).

-

(
\mathsf{Frame}
). We are asked to show that
\models\left\{{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right\}
under the assumptions:

-

(H1).
{\vdash\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}}
,

Applying the IH to (H1), we conclude
\models\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}
(H2). The claim is:

\forall\mathcal{R}\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket.\left({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right)\Rightarrow\left(\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket({{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}})\models{({(\psi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}\right)

Fix
\mathcal{R}
and assume
{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}\models{({(\phi)^{\Xi}}*{(\xi)^{\Theta}})^{\Delta}}
. From this assumption, we deduce that there are:

{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}\models{(\phi)^{\Xi}}

and that

{{(\mathcal{T})^{\Theta}}}\models{(\xi)^{\Theta}}

such that
{{(\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{T})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}}\approx\sqsubseteq{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}
, which is equivalent to:

{{(\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{T})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}}\approx{{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta}.

Observe that for (H2)
\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}
. From these observations, we know that
\llbracket\Xi\Join\Theta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket
is defined. From
Lemma

10
, we deduce that:

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{T})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}})}\approx\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}}_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta})}.

From this result and
Lemma

6
, we conclude that

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{T})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}})}\approx\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Theta}},

which means

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{T})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}})}\approx\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})}.

By applying
Lemma

9
, we observe that
\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{T})^{\Xi\Join\Theta}}})}=\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}})}\otimes{{(\mathcal{T})^{\Theta}}}
, so:

\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}})}\otimes{{(\mathcal{T})^{\Theta}}}\approx\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathtt{P}\rrbracket{({{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}})}.

With (C1-2), and by applying (H2) to
{{(\mathcal{Q})^{\Xi}}}
, we conclude the proof.

-

(
\mathsf{Restr}
). We want to show that
\models\left\{{(\phi)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Delta}}\right\}
under the assumptions:

-

(H1).
{\vdash\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}}
,

-

(H2).
{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\sqsubseteq{(\phi)^{\Delta}}
.

-

(H3).
{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\sqsubseteq{(\psi)^{\Delta}}
.

For IH and (H1), we know that
\models{\left\{{(\phi)^{\Xi}}\right\}\;\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\;\left\{{(\psi)^{\Xi}}\right\}}
holds. Les
s
be a state such that:

s\models^{\Delta}{(\phi)^{\Delta}}.

From (H2) or (H3) and
Remark

4
we can deduce
\Xi\sqsubseteq\Delta
.
This means that
s_{\Delta\to\Xi}
is defined, and for

Lemma

23
and (H2), we have:

s_{\Delta\to\Xi}\models^{\Xi}{(\phi)^{\Xi}}.

From the IH, we conclude
\llbracket\Xi\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi})\models^{\Xi}{(\psi)^{\Xi}}
. From
Lemma

6
,
we conclude

{\llbracket\Delta\vdash\mathtt{P}\rrbracket(s)}_{\Delta\to\Xi}\models^{\Xi}{(\psi)^{\Xi}}.

We show the claim with another application of
Lemma

23
, which is justified by (H3).

∎

6
Some Examples

This section is devoted to giving two simple well-known cryptographic
constructions which can be proved to have the desired properties by way of
CSL.

6.1
The Pseudo One-Time Pad

\vdash\left\{\top\right\}\;\Delta\vdash k\leftarrow\mathit{rnd}();c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))\;\left\{{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}*{(\mathbf{U}(c))^{\Delta_{c}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\pi_{\mathtt{POTP}}\vdash\left\{{{\mathbf{U}(k)}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))\;\left\{{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}*{\mathbf{U}(c)}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{{\mathbf{U}(g(k))}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))\;\left\{{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}*{\mathbf{U}(c)}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{(\top)^{\Delta}}\land{({\mathbf{U}(k)}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta_{k,m}}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))\;\left\{{(\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))))^{{\Delta}}}\land{({\mathbf{U}(g(k))}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta_{k,m}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{\top\right\}\;{\Delta}\vdash c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))\;\left\{\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(m,g(k)))\right\}c\notin\mathsf{FV}(\mathit{xor}(m,g(k)))\{m,k\}\cap\mathsf{MV}(c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(k)))=\emptyset\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:prekm}, Axiom \eqref{ax:auxpotp2}}\eqref{eq:axpotp}

Where
\pi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
is:

\vdash\left\{\top\right\}\;\Delta\vdash k\leftarrow\mathit{rnd}()\;\left\{{\mathbf{U}(k)}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{(\top)^{\{\}}}*{(\top)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash k\leftarrow\mathit{rnd}()\;\left\{{{(\top\land\mathbf{EQ}(k,\mathit{rnd}()))^{\Delta_{k}}}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m,c}}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\varepsilon\vdash\mathit{rnd}:\mathsf{Str}[n]r\notin\mathsf{FV}(\mathit{rnd}){(\top)^{\Delta_{c,m}}}\in\Phi r\notin\mathsf{dom}(\varepsilon)\text{Axiom \eqref{ax:auxpotp1}}

Figure 8:
Proof tree of
\vdash\left\{\phi_{\mathtt{POTP}}\right\}\;\mathtt{POTP}\;\left\{\psi_{\mathtt{POTP}}\right\}
.

The pseudo-one-time pad (
\mathtt{POTP}
) is a symmetric key encryption
scheme that enjoys
computational secrecy

[
28
,
20
]
.
It differs from the classic one-time pad because exclusive or is not applied to the key
itself but rather to the output of a

pseudorandom generator
when applied to the actual key. This solves the
main drawback of
perfectly secret
encryption schemes: with
\mathtt{POTP}
, the
cardinality of the key space can be strictly smaller than that of the message space.

It is possible to use our logic to show that the
\mathtt{POTP}

scheme enjoys computational secrecy.
Analogously to what we did in
Section

2
we express the encryption
procedure in our language as the following program, that we call

\mathtt{POTP}
:

\displaystyle k\leftarrow\mathit{rnd}();

\displaystyle c\leftarrow\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))

After having generated the key
k
by way of
\mathit{rnd}()
, the latter is passed to
a pseudorandom generator
g(\cdot)
, and only at that point the latter is xored
with the message
m
. In the proof, the fact that
g
is a pseudorandom
generator is captured by the following axiom:

\displaystyle{(\mathbf{U}(x))^{\Delta}}\models{(\mathbf{U}(g(x)))^{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\text{if }\Delta(x)=\mathsf{Str}[n]

(
\text{Ax}_{\mathtt{POTP}}
)

Although apparently stronger than the textbook definition for
pseudorandom generators where
\mathbf{U}(x)
is replaced by

\mathbf{EQ}(x,\mathit{rnd}())
,
\text{Ax}_{\mathtt{POTP}}
is actually equivalent to it.
1
1

1

This happens because if
\text{Ax}_{\mathtt{POTP}}
did not follow from
the standard version of the axiom, then there would be a store

{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\llbracket{(\mathbf{U}(x))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket{(\mathbf{EQ}(x,\mathit{rnd}()))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket

such that
\llbracket{(g(x))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
is
not
pseudorandom.
In particular, there would be an adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
with
non-negligible advantage in distinguishing

g(x)
from the uniform distribution.
But this would contradict
{(\mathcal{R})^{\Delta}}\in\llbracket{(\mathbf{U}(x))^{\Delta}}\rrbracket
, because
the distinguisher
\lambda y,z.{\mathcal{A}}(y,\llparenthesis g(z)\rrparenthesis)
would have
non-negligible advantage on
\llbracket x\rrbracket(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[n])
.

The next step is to find a way to capture the desired property as a Hoare triple.
The program we want to prove secure is certainly
\mathtt{POTP}
, but how about pre-
and post-conditions? Traditionally, computational secrecy is captured by a form
of indistinguishability, which requires the introduction of an experiment. As
suggested by Yao
[
3
]
, however, we can stay closer to Shannon’s
perfect security, and rather adopt a weaker notion of independence. More
specifically, we pick a post-condition stating that the message and the
ciphertext are computationally independent. Formally,
we can take
\phi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
as precondition and
\psi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
as postcondition,
as follows:

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathtt{POTP}}

\displaystyle={(\top)^{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathtt{POTP}}

\displaystyle={({(\top)^{m:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]}}*{\mathbf{U}(c)})^{\Delta}},

with
\Delta={{k}:\mathsf{Str}[n],{m},{c},r:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]}
.
Observe that

\psi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
requires
\mathbf{U}(c)
, which means that

c
is not only computational independent from
m
, but also
indistinguishable from the uniform distribution, while in principle it
would be sufficient to show a weaker statement instead,
namely:
{({(\top)^{m:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]}}*{(\top)^{c:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]}})^{{k}:\mathsf{Str}[n],{m},{c}:\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]}}
.
Proving that this is equivalent to indistinguishability for our notion of
computational independence is not trivial, and
Section

7
is devoted
to showing it.

Proving the validity
of the triple

\models\left\{\phi_{\mathtt{POTP}}\right\}\;\mathtt{POTP}\;\left\{\psi_{\mathtt{POTP}}\right\}

by hand is cumbersome and error-prone. Conversely, it is possible
to give the proof in a more structured and concise way by employing
the inference rules we defined in
Section

5
. It is remarkable that the
proof above is not based on any form of reduction, being solely based on a form
of program analysis, which proceeds compositionally without even
mentioning the adversary.
The complete proof tree is in
Figure

8
; it starts with
an application of the
\mathsf{Seq}
rule to synchronize the
triple obtained for the first command (in the subtree
\pi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
)
to the one obtained for the second one. In
\pi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
, the rule

\mathsf{SRAssn}
plays the important role of moving
k
from the environment
to the right of the separating conjunction to that on the left.
This way, the post-condition states that
k
is uniform
and that it is computationally independent from all the other
variables in the environment. Then, by means of the
\mathsf{Weak}
rule,
we rewrite the pre- and the post-conditions: on the left-hand side,
we are rewriting a tautology to another tautology,
while the rewriting on the right is valid of the following axiom:

{(\top\land\mathbf{EQ}(k,\mathit{rnd}()))^{\Delta_{k}}}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m,c}}}\models^{\Delta}\mathbf{U}(k)*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}

(3)

For the second command, we start by applying the
\mathsf{DAssn}
rule,
which shows that in every sample in the support of the
final state, the value of
c
is identical to that of
\mathit{xor}(m,g(k))
.
Since the variables
m
and
k
are not modified by the second assignment,
we can apply the
\mathsf{Const}
rule to add to both the sides
any formula
{(\phi)^{\Delta_{m,k}}}
, where

\Delta_{m,k}
denotes the restriction to the domain
\{m,k\}
.
In particular, we add
{({\mathbf{U}(k)}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta_{k,m}}}
, which is very close to post-condition of the triple
we showed in
\pi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
. We chose this formula, because one of our goals
is to obtain the post-condition of
\pi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
as a pre-condition
in order to apply the
\mathsf{Seq}
rule.
With the next application of the
\mathsf{Weak}
rule, we rewrite the
pre-condition with a stronger formula — the modification to the environment of

{({\mathbf{U}(k)}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta_{k,m}}}
is justified by
Lemma

23
. On the post-condition, we apply
the following axiom schema:

\begin{gathered}{{({(\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(m,d)))^{{\Xi\Join\Delta_{c,m}}}}\land{({(\mathbf{U}(d))^{\Xi}}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta}})^{\Delta}}}\\
\hfill\models^{\Delta}{{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}*{(\mathbf{U}(c))^{\Delta_{c}}}},\end{gathered}

(4)

that allows to conclude that
c
is presudorandom
under the assumption that the expression
d
is
also pseudorandom.
In the very last application of the
\mathsf{Weak}
rule, we apply the
(

Ax
POTP

) to the pre-condition in order to deduce
\mathbf{U}(g(k))
from

\mathbf{U}(k)
; this way our final pre-condition matches exactly the post condition of

\pi_{\mathtt{POTP}}
, and we can conclude the proof by applying the
\mathsf{Seq}
rule.
The validity of axioms (
3
) and (
4
) is shown
in
Lemmas

29
and
6
below.

Lemma 29
.

For every,
\Delta
, such that
\Delta(\{m,c\})=\{\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]\}
,

\Delta(k)=\{\mathsf{Str}[n]\}
, every
\Xi
such that

\Xi\Join\Delta_{m,c}\downarrow
and

\Xi\Join\Delta_{m,c}\sqsubseteq\Delta
,
and every
s\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket
, if
s
satisfies

{({(\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(m,d)))^{{\Xi\Join\Delta_{c,m}}}}\land{({(\mathbf{U}(d))^{\Xi}}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta}})^{\Delta}}

then it satisfies:

{({(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}*{(\mathbf{U}(c))^{\Delta_{c}}})^{\Delta}}.

Proof.

From the assumption, we deduce:

-

(H1).
s_{\Delta\to{\Xi\Join\Delta_{c,m}}}\models\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(m,d))

-

(H2).
s\models{({(\mathbf{U}(d))^{\Xi}}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta}}

The assumption (H2) allows us to deduce

-

(H2A).
t_{1}\models{(\mathbf{U}(d))^{\Xi}}

-

(H2B).
t_{2}\models{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}

-

(H2C).
{t_{1}\otimes{t_{2}}}\approx\sqsubseteq{s}

The goal is to find
u_{1},u_{2}
such that:

-

(C1).
u_{1}\models{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}}

-

(C2).
u_{2}\models{(\mathbf{U}(c))^{\Delta_{c}}}

-

(C3).
u_{1}\otimes u_{2}\approx s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{m,c}}
.

Where, for stating (C3), we have expanded the definition of
\approx\sqsubseteq
.
We set
u_{1}=t_{2}
and
u_{2}=s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{c}}
.
With these choices (C1) is trivial. We go for (C2).
This claim requires us to show that:

\llbracket c\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{c}})\approx\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)])

From (H1) and (W2), we know that

\llbracket c\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m,c}})=\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,d)\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m,c}})

From Lemma
5
, we deduce that:

\llbracket c\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{c}})=\llbracket c\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m,c}}),

so, for this observation, we can reduce (C2) to:

\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,d)\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m,c}})\approx\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]),

And by applying Lemma
5
once again, to:

\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,d)\rrbracket(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m}})\approx\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]).

Moreover, from (H2C), we observe that
s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m}}\approx t_{1}\otimes t_{2}
so, with an application of Lemma
2
,
(C2) can be restated as follows:

\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,d)\rrbracket(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})\approx\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]).

Now, we assume without lack of generality that the function symbols also contain a
primitive
\mathit{rnd}(\tau)
for every type
\tau
that generates a
binary string of length
q(n)
uniformly at random whenever

\tau=\mathsf{Str}[q(n)]
. Observe that:

\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,\mathit{rnd}(p(n)))\rrbracket(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})=\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)]).

This can be shown with the same argument used to
show the correctness of the OTP encryption scheme.
For this reason, (C2) can be reduced to:

\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,d)\rrbracket(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})\approx\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,\mathit{rnd}(p(n)))\rrbracket(t_{1}\otimes t_{2}).

assume that this claim does not hold. This means that there is a distinguisher

{\mathcal{A}}
with non-negligible advantage for these two distribution ensembles.
Also observe that
t_{1}\otimes t_{2}
is efficiently samplable,
that
g
is poly-time and that also
\mathit{rnd}(p(n))
is efficiently samplable.
It is possible to define a distinguisher
{\mathcal{B}}
for
\llbracket d\rrbracket(t_{1})

and the family of
uniform distributions
\{\mathsf{unif}(p)(n)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
This distinguisher receives in input a sample
string
x
, it samples
t_{1}\otimes t_{2}
to obtain a map
l
,
it computes
l(m)
, and returns the result of the
\mathit{xor}
between this value and
x

to
{\mathcal{A}}
. Observe that if the input is sampled according to
\llbracket d\rrbracket(t_{1})
, the distribution
of the inputs for
{\mathcal{A}}
will be that of
\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,d)\rrbracket(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})
,
conversely, if it is sampled according to the uniform distribution, it
will be that of
\llbracket\mathit{xor}(m,\mathit{rnd}(p(n)))\rrbracket(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})
.
For this reason the advantage of
{\mathcal{B}}
is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}
, in particular,
it is non-negligible. This is absurd because we are assuming that

t_{1}\models\mathbf{U}(d)
(H2A).
It remains just to show (C3). This claim can be rewritten
by inlining
the definitions of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
for more
clarity:

t_{2}\otimes s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{c}}\approx s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{m,c}}.

For compatibility of
\approx
and
\otimes
(
Lemma

19
), we show:

t_{2}\otimes u\approx s_{\Delta\to\Delta_{m,c}}.

where
u
is the distribution defined on
c
as the family of uniform distribution
of strings of length
p(n)
.
Assume that this does not hold. This means that there is a distinguisher
{\mathcal{A}}

with non-negligible advantage for these two distributions.
Out of it, it is possible to define another distinguisher
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
for
the following efficiently samplable distribution ensemble on pairs:

\text{bind}(t_{2}\otimes u,l\mapsto{\text{unit}((l(m),\mathit{xor}(l(m),l(c))))})

and

\text{bind}(s_{m,c},l\mapsto{\text{unit}((l(m),\mathit{xor}(l(m),l(c))))}).

Indeed,
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
can simply take its input sample
x
,
compute the bitwise exclusive or on the first component (
x_{1}
)
and the second component of
x
(
x_{2}
) and call that value
y
,
then,
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
calls
{\mathcal{A}}
on the sample where
m
is associated to

x_{1}
and
c
is associated to
y
. It is easy to see that the
advantage of
{\mathcal{A}}
is the same of
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
.
In particular, observe that (D1) is equal to:

\text{bind}(t_{2},w\mapsto\text{bind}(u,l\mapsto{\text{unit}((w(m),\mathit{xor}(w(m),l(c))))})),

which, in turn, is equal to:

\text{bind}(t_{2},w\mapsto\text{bind}(u,l\mapsto{\text{unit}((w(m),l(c)))})).

Because
l(c)
is statistically uniform.
From (H1), we deduce that (D2) is equal to:

\text{bind}(s,l\mapsto{\text{unit}((l(m),\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis(l)))}),

and thus to:

\text{bind}(s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m}},l\mapsto{\text{unit}((l(m),\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis(l)))}),

for
Remark

2
. Due to (H2C), we know that
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
has negligible advantage also
in distinguishing (D2), from:

\text{bind}(t_{1}\otimes t_{2},l\mapsto{\text{unit}((l(m),\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis(l)))}),

This is shown by contraposition: any distinguisher
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}

that succeeds in distinguishing (D2) and (D3) with
non-negligible advantage can be used to define a distinguisher
\overline{{\mathcal{B}}}

for
s_{\Delta\to\Xi\Join\Delta_{m}}
and,

t_{1}\otimes t_{2}
. The distinguisher
\overline{{\mathcal{B}}}

simply computes
(l(m),\llparenthesis d\rrparenthesis_{n}(l))

on its input sample and passes it to
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
The distinguishers
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
\overline{{\mathcal{B}}}

have the same advantage, and this contradicts (H2C).
This shows that (D3) and (D2) are computationally indistinguishable.
So, for the triangular inequality, and by observing that the sum of a negligible
function with another negligible function is itself a negligible function,
it must be the case where the advantage of
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
in distinguishing (D1)
and (D3) is non-negligible.

Now, we observe that (D1) is equal to:

\text{bind}(t_{2},w\mapsto\text{bind}(u,l\mapsto{\text{unit}((w(m),l(c)))})),

and to:

\text{bind}(t_{1}\otimes t_{2},w\mapsto\text{bind}(u,l\mapsto{\text{unit}((w(m),l(c)))})).

This is a contradiction: it is possible to define a new adversary
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
out of

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
with non-negligible advantage on
\llbracket d\rrbracket(t_{1})
and
the uniform distribution of binary strings with length
p(n)
.
This adversary receives in input its sample
x
, then it samples from

t_{1}\otimes t_{2}
a sample
l
and then gives the
pair
(l(m),\mathit{xor}({l(m)},x))
in input to
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
.
Observe that, if the sample is
taken from
\llbracket d\rrbracket(t_{1})
, then
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}

gives the same output
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
gave for (D3), while if
the input sample
x
was distributed uniformly,
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}

gives the same output that
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
gave for (D1).
The advantage of
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
is non-negligible,
but this contradicts (H2A).
∎

Remark 6
.

{({(\mathbf{EQ}(k,\mathit{rnd}()))^{\Delta_{k}}}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m,c}}})^{\Delta}}\models{({(\mathbf{U}(k))^{\Delta_{k}}}*{(\top)^{\Delta_{m}}})^{\Delta}}

Proof.

Assume that a state
r
satisfies the formula on the left. There are
r,t
such that
r\otimes t\downarrow
and in particular
r\models\mathbf{EQ}(k,\mathit{rnd}())
, so the claim follows trivially from (W1) and (U1). Observe that the second sub-formula of consequence is not identical to that of the premise, but this is sound because of
Lemma

23
.
∎

6.2
Bitwise Exclusive Or

\begin{aligned} \vdash\left\{{(\top)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{if}\;k=1\;\mathtt{then}\;c\leftarrow\lnot m\;\mathtt{else}\;c\leftarrow m\;\left\{{(\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(k,m)))^{\Delta}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{(\mathbf{IS}(k,1))^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash c\leftarrow\lnot k\;\left\{{(\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(k,m)))^{\Delta}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{({(\top)^{\Delta}}\land{(\mathbf{IS}(k,1))^{\Delta_{k}}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash c\leftarrow\lnot k\;\left\{{({(\mathbf{IS}(c,\lnot m))^{\Delta}}\land{(\mathbf{IS}(k,1))^{\Delta_{k}}})^{\Delta}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash\left\{{(\top)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash c\leftarrow\lnot k\;\left\{{(\mathbf{IS}(c,\lnot m))^{\Delta}}\right\}c\notin\mathsf{FV}(\lnot k)\{k\}\cap\mathsf{MV}(c\leftarrow\lnot k)=\emptyset\text{Remarks \ref{rem:pi1} and \ref{rem:pi2}}\theta\mathbf{IS}(c,\mathit{xor}(k,m))\in{\Phi_{\mathsf{ex}}}\end{aligned}

Figure 9:
Example of employment of the
\mathsf{RCond}
rule for the
\mathtt{XOR}
program.

In this section we show how our program logic can be employed to show the
correctness of the following program computing the exclusive or of two bits.

\mathtt{XOR}\mathrel{:=}\mathtt{if}\;k=1\;\mathtt{then}\;c\leftarrow\lnot m\;\mathtt{else}\;c\leftarrow m.

We do so by showing the correctness of the triple:

\vdash\left\{{(\top)^{\Delta}}\right\}\;\Delta\vdash\mathtt{XOR}\;\left\{{(\mathbf{EQ}(c,\mathit{xor}(k,m)))^{\Delta}}\right\},

where
\Delta=c,k,m:\mathsf{Bool}
.
The full tree is in
Figure

9
. We omit the subtree
\theta
because it is analogous to the one on its left. Actually, the claim we are showing is stronger that the one in the triple, but this last one can be obtained by an application of the
\mathsf{Weak}
rule with the (W2) axiom.

Remark 7
.

{(\mathbf{IS}(k,1))^{\Delta}}\models{({(\top)^{\Delta}}\land{(\mathbf{IS}(k,1))^{\Delta_{k}}})^{\Delta}}

Proof.

With
Lemma

3
, we show the validity of
{(\mathbf{EQ}(k,1))^{\Delta_{k}}}
; that of
{(\top)^{\Delta}}
is trivial.
∎

Remark 8
.

{({(\mathbf{EQ}(c,\lnot m))^{\Delta}}\land{(\mathbf{EQ}(k,1))^{\Delta_{k}}})^{\Delta}}\models{(\mathbf{EQ}(c,\mathit{xor}(k,m)))^{\Delta}}

Proof.

Observe that:

\models^{\Delta}{(\mathbf{EQ}(\lnot m,\mathit{xor}(m,1)))^{\Delta}}

The claim follows from two applications of (T2).
∎

6.3
Pseudorandom Key Stretching

In this section we employ our program logic to show that
if pseudorandom generators with expansion factor
n+1
exist,
then pseudo-random generators with expansion factor
n+h+1\geq n+1
exist as well
for any constant
h\in\mathbb{N}
.
Even though this result by itself is not particularly insightful and is
superseded by classic results about pseudorandom generators
[
20
]
,
we believe it is anyway interesting, given the style of the resulting proof.

To this end, we define the family of programs
\{\mathtt{EXP}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
where each program
\mathtt{EXP}_{h}
with
h\in\mathbb{N}

is a key stretching algorithm with expansion factor
n+h+1
.
The construction of these algorithms is based on that of the canonical
pseudo-random generator with expansion factor
n+p(n)

starting from a pseudo-random generator with expansion factor

n+1
, see
[
20
]
.
We define the pseudorandom generator
\{\mathtt{EXP}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathtt{EXP}_{h}\mathrel{:=}

\displaystyle\mathtt{P}_{0};\ldots;\mathtt{P}_{h};s_{0}\leftarrow k;\mathtt{R}_{0};\ldots;\mathtt{R}_{h}

where, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{P}_{i}\mathrel{:=}

\displaystyle r_{i}\leftarrow g(k);b_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{head}({r_{i}});k\leftarrow\mathit{tail}({r_{i}});

\displaystyle\mathtt{R}_{i}\mathrel{:=}

\displaystyle s_{i+1}\leftarrow\mathit{concat}_{n+i,1}({s_{i}},{b_{i}})

The claim we are interested in showing states that, if the
distribution of
k
in the initial store
is pseudo-random, then the distribution of
s_{h+1}
in the final
store is also pseudo-random.
This can be expressed by the following triple:

\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\top^{\Xi_{0}}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{EXP}_{h}\;\left\{\mathbf{U}(s_{h+1})\right\},

where, for every
h\in n
, the environment
\Delta_{h}
is defined
the environment
\Delta
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta_{h}(k)&=\mathsf{Str}[n]&\forall 0\leq i\leq h.\Delta_{h}(r_{i})&=\mathsf{Str}[n+1]\\
\forall 0\leq i\leq h.\Delta_{h}(b_{i})&=\mathsf{Bool}&\Delta_{h}({s_{i}})(k)&=\mathsf{Str}[n+i].\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\Delta_{h}(s_{h+1})=\mathsf{Str}[n+h+1]

and for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the environment
\Xi_{i}

is equal to
\Delta_{h}
, but all the
r_{j}
and
b_{j}

for
0\leq j<i
are undefined.

The proof relies on the axiom (

Ax
POTP

) and on two other
auxiliary axioms (

Ax
SPL

) and (

Ax
MRG

). The
former states that if in a store
s
the variable
r

is distributed uniformly at random, the variable
b
stores the first bit
of
r
, and the variable
s
stores the remaining part of the
string, then the marginal distributions of
b
and
s

are pseudorandom and independent.
The axiom (

Ax
MRG

) somehow states the converse:
by concatenating a bit and a string that are both pseudorandom and
conditionally independent, we obtain a string that is pseudorandom.
These axioms can be given in our language as follows:

\begin{multlined}{{({(\mathbf{U}(r))^{\Xi}}\land{(\mathbf{IS}(b,\mathit{head}(r)))^{\Xi}}\land{(\mathbf{IS}(s,\mathit{tail}(r)))^{\Xi}})^{\Xi}}}\models^{\Xi}{{(\mathbf{U}(b)*\mathbf{U}(s))^{\Xi}}}\end{multlined}

(
\text{Ax}_{\mathtt{SPL}}
)

(\mathbf{U}(r)*\mathbf{U}(b))\land\mathbf{IS}(s,\mathit{concat}(r,b))\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{U}(s)

(
\text{Ax}_{\mathtt{MRG}}
)

Where
\Xi(b)=\mathsf{Bool},\Xi(s)=\mathsf{Str}[p(n)],\Xi(r)=\mathsf{Str}[p(n)+1]
.

Lemma 30
.

The axioms (

Ax
SPL

) and (

Ax
MRG

) are sound.
∎

Proof.

We start by showing the (

Ax
SPL

) axiom:

{(\mathbf{U}(r)\land\mathbf{IS}(b,\mathit{head}(r))\land\mathbf{IS}(s,\mathit{tail}(r)))^{\Xi}}\models^{\Xi}{(\mathbf{U}(b)*\mathbf{U}(s))^{\Xi}}

The goal is to show:

-

(C1).
t_{b}\models^{\Xi_{b}}\mathbf{U}(b)

-

(C2).
t_{s}\models^{\Xi_{s}}\mathbf{U}(s)

-

(C3).
t_{b}\otimes t_{s}\approx t_{b,s}

Under the assumption:

s\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{U}(r)\land\mathbf{IS}(b,\mathit{head}(r))\land\mathbf{IS}(s,\mathit{tail}(r)),

i.e.:

-

(H1).
t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{U}(r)

-

(H2).
t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{IS}(b,\mathit{head}(r))

-

(H3).
t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{IS}(s,\mathit{tail}(r))

We start by showing (C1).
From (W1) and (W2), we deduce that
t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{CI}(b,\mathit{head}(r))
.
We start the following intermediate result:

t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{U}(\mathit{head}(r)).

Assume this
to be false. This means that there is an adversary
{\mathcal{A}}
that
distinguishes samples of the distribution ensemble
\llbracket head(r)\rrbracket(t)

from the samples of the distribution ensemble
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Bool})
. Out of this distinguisher,
it is possible to define a new distinguisher
{\mathcal{B}}
for the distribution ensembles

\llbracket r\rrbracket(t_{r})
and
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[n])
.
The adversary
{\mathcal{B}}
receives in input a sample
x
and
1^{n}
, then
it computes
b^{\prime}=\mathit{head}(x)
and returns
{\mathcal{A}}(b^{\prime},1^{n})
. Observe that
if the input of
{\mathcal{A}}
follows the distribution
\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t_{r})
,
then the output of
{\mathcal{B}}
is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}
when its input is sampled
from
\llbracket\mathit{head}(r)\rrbracket_{n}(t)
, because for
Lemma

5

\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t_{r})
=
\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t)
; similarly, if the input is taken from

\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[n])
, then the output of
{\mathcal{B}}
is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}

when the input is sampled from
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Bool})
. In particular, the
advantage of
{\mathcal{B}}
is the same of
{\mathcal{A}}
,
but this contradicts (H1). So, it must be the case where

t\models^{\Xi}\mathsf{unif}(\mathit{head}(r))
,
For axiom (U1) and
t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{CI}(b,\mathit{head}(r))
,
we deduce
t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{U}(b)
.
From
Lemma

5
, we deduce that

\llbracket b\rrbracket(t_{b})=\llbracket b\rrbracket(t)
.
From this observation, we conclude
t_{b}\models^{\Xi_{b}}\mathbf{U}(b)
.
The proof of (C2) is analogous to that of (C1).
Now, we show (C3).
Assume that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a distinguisher for
t_{b}\otimes t_{s}

and
t_{b,s}
with non-negligible advantage.
Due to compatibility of
\otimes
and
\approx
,
Lemma

19
,
{\mathcal{A}}

must have non-negligible advantage also in distinguishing

u_{b}\otimes u_{s}
and
t_{b,s}
, where:

•

u_{b}
is the efficiently samplable distribution ensemble such that
for every
n
,
(u_{b})_{n}=\{\{b\mapsto 0\}^{\frac{1}{2}},\{b\mapsto 1\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\}
.

•

u_{s}
is the efficiently samplable distribution ensemble such that
for every
n
,

(u_{s})_{n}=\{\{s\mapsto z\}^{2^{-n}},\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\}
.

The distinguisher
{\mathcal{A}}
can be used to define a
new adversary
{\mathcal{B}}
that distinguishes
the distribution
\llbracket r\rrbracket(t)
from
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[n+1])
.
The distinguisher
{\mathcal{B}}
takes in input
its sample and the security parameter
1^{n}
, then it produces the store:

(m_{x})_{n}=\{b\mapsto\mathit{head}(x),s\mapsto\mathit{tail}(x)\}

and returns
{\mathcal{A}}((m_{x})_{n},1^{n})
.
If
x
is sampled according to
\llbracket r\rrbracket(t)
, we
observe that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the samples
(m_{x})_{n}

follow exactly the distribution
(t_{b,s})_{n}
.
This holds because for every
n
, thanks to (H2) and (H3),
we have:

t_{n}=\{\{r\mapsto z,b\mapsto\mathit{head}(z),s\mapsto tail(z)\}^{p_{z}},\text{for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n+1}\},

where
\{p_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}
is an indexed family of non-negative reals
such that
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n+1}}p_{x}=1
. For this reason, we deduce that:

\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t)=\{\{z^{n+1}\}^{p_{z}},\text{for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n+1}\}

So, if the sample
x
is taken from the distribution
\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t)
,
the distribution of the samples that are given in input
to
{\mathcal{A}}
, is:
F(\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t))
where

F(z)=\{b\mapsto head(z),s\mapsto tail(z)\}
. Since
F
is an injection, we have that:

F(\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t))=\{\{b\mapsto head(z),s\mapsto tail(z)\}^{p_{z}},\text{for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n+1}\},

which is exactly
(t_{b,s})_{n}
.
For a similar reason, we observe that
if
x
is sampled according to
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[n+1])
, the inputs
of
{\mathcal{A}}
follow the distribution
(u_{b}\otimes u_{s})_{n}
.
This shows that
{\mathcal{B}}
succeeds in distinguishing
\llbracket r\rrbracket_{u}

from
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[n+1])
with non-negligible advantage, but this is
a contradiction of the assumption
t\models^{\Xi}\mathsf{unif}(r)
.
This shows
u_{b}\otimes u_{s}\approx t_{b,s}
, which was our goal.
Now, we show axiom (

Ax
MRG

). The goal is to show:

t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{U}(s)

Under the assumption:

t\models^{\Xi}({(\mathbf{U}(r))^{\Xi_{r}}}*{(\mathbf{U}(b))^{\Xi_{b}}})\land{(\mathbf{IS}(s,\mathit{concat}(r,b)))^{\Xi}}

Which means that there are
t_{1},t_{2}
such that:

-

(H1).
{t_{1}\models^{\Xi_{r}}\mathbf{U}(r)}

-

(H2).
{t_{2}\models^{\Xi_{b}}\mathbf{U}(b)}

-

(H3).
{{t_{1}}\otimes{t_{2}}\approx t_{r,b}}

Let
u_{1},u_{2}
be the distribution ensembles defined as follows:

\displaystyle(u_{1})_{n}

\displaystyle=\{\{r\mapsto z\}^{2^{-p(n)}},\text{for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\}

\displaystyle(u_{2})_{n}

\displaystyle=\{\{b\mapsto 0\}^{\frac{1}{2}},\{b\mapsto 1\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\}

Observe that
u_{1}\approx t_{1}
and
u_{2}\approx t_{2}
. We show this
result only in the case of
u_{1}
and
t_{1}
, that of
u_{2}
and
t_{2}

is analogous.

Assume that there is a distinguisher
{\mathcal{A}}
for
u_{1}
and
t_{1}
. We can
use this distinguisher to define a new distinguisher
{\mathcal{B}}
for

\llbracket r\rrbracket(t_{1})
and
\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)+1])
. This adversary
takes in input a sample
x
and
1^{n}
and returns

{\mathcal{A}}(\{r\mapsto x\},1^{n})
.
If
x
is sampled uniformly, then the distribution of
{\mathcal{A}}
’s inputs
is
\{\{r\mapsto z\}^{2^{-n}},\text{for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\}
, i.e.
u_{1}
.
If the input is sampled according to
\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t_{1})
, we observe that:

(t_{1})=\{\{r\mapsto z\}^{p_{z}}\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\}

where
\{p_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}
is a family of non-negative reals such that

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}p_{r}=1
. We observe that:

\llbracket r\rrbracket_{n}(t_{1})=\{z^{p_{z}}\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\}

because the function
\{k\mapsto z\}\mapsto z
is a bijection. For the same reason,
the inputs of
{\mathcal{A}}
follow exactly the distribution
t_{1}
. This shows that the
adversary
{\mathcal{B}}
has the same advantage of
{\mathcal{A}}
, but this contradicts the
assumption
t_{1}\models^{\Xi_{r}}\mathbf{U}(r)
, so we conclude

t_{1}\approx u_{1}
. Using a similar argument on
t_{2}
and
u_{2}
, we
can apply compatibility to show:
u_{1}\otimes u_{2}\approx t_{r,b}

From this result and
Lemma

2
, we deduce that:

\llbracket\mathit{concat}(r,b)\rrbracket(t_{r,b})\approx\llbracket\mathit{concat}(r,b)\rrbracket(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})

In particular, we observe that

\llbracket\mathit{concat}(r,b)\rrbracket(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})=\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)+1])

by definition of
\llbracket\mathit{concat}\rrbracket(r,b){}
. This means that

\llbracket\mathit{concat}(r,b)\rrbracket(t_{r,b})\approx\mathsf{unif}(\mathsf{Str}[p(n)+1])
, which entails:

t_{r,b}\models^{\Xi_{r,b}}\mathbf{U}(\mathit{concat}(r,b)).

From this result and
Corollary

1
, we deduce:

t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{U}(\mathit{concat}(r,b)).

From axioms (W1-2), we deduce
t\models^{\Xi}\mathbf{CI}(s,\mathit{concat}(r,b))
, and
with an application of axiom (U1), we conclude
t\models^{\Xi}\mathsf{unif}(s)
.
∎

We start by showing
the construction of the trees
\pi_{i}
such that:

\vdash\left\{{(\phi_{i}^{h})^{\Delta_{h}}}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{P}_{i}\;\left\{{(\phi_{i+1}^{h})^{\Delta_{h}}}\right\}

where

\displaystyle\phi_{i}^{h}

\displaystyle={(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\;\;\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}

So, for
i=h+1
, this corresponds to showing the triple:

\vdash\left\{{(\phi_{0}^{h})^{\Delta_{h}}}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{P}_{h}\;\left\{{(\phi_{h+1}^{h})^{\Delta_{h}}}\right\}.

Observe that
\phi_{h+1}^{h}
corresponds to:

{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\;\;\Sep_{j=0}^{h}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}.

The triple

\vdash\left\{\mathbf{U}(k)\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{P}_{0};\ldots;\mathtt{P}_{h}\;\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*{\Sep_{j=0}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right\}

can thus be obtained from
\pi_{h}
with an application of the
\mathsf{Weak}
rule and multiple applications of the
\mathsf{Seq}
rule. In particular, with the
\mathsf{Weak}
rule, we observe

\mathbf{U}(k)\models^{\Delta_{h}}\mathbf{U}(k)*{{(\top)^{\varepsilon}}}=\phi^{h}_{0}

With application of the rule
\mathsf{SDAssn}
, we can obtain the triple

\displaystyle\vdash\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*{\Sep_{j=0}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\Delta_{h}\vdash s_{0}\leftarrow k

\displaystyle\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(k)\land\mathbf{IS}(s_{0},k))^{k,s_{0}:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*{\Sep_{j=0}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right\}

By means of the
\mathsf{Weak}
rule and axioms (W1), (W2) and (U1), we can rewrite the conclusion as follows:

{(\mathbf{U}(s_{0}))^{k,s_{0}:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*{\Sep_{j=0}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}
.
Finally, with
Lemma

23
and
Lemma

22
we can simplify the conclusion one further step, to obtain:

\displaystyle\vdash\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*{\Sep_{j=0}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash s_{0}\leftarrow k\;\left\{\left({(\mathbf{U}(s_{0}))^{s_{0}:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}\right)*\left({\Sep_{j=0}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}

Observe that this can be put in a chain of applications of the
\mathsf{Seq}

rule to show:

\vdash\left\{\mathbf{U}(k)\right\}\\
\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{P}_{0};\ldots;\mathtt{P}_{h};s_{0}\leftarrow k\\
\left\{\left({(\mathbf{U}(s_{0}))^{s_{0}:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}\right)*\left({\Sep_{j=0}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}

We continue by defining the formula
\psi_{i}^{h}
as follows:

\psi_{i}^{h}\mathrel{:=}\left({(\mathbf{U}(s_{i}))^{s_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i]}}\right)*\left({\Sep_{j=i}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)

Then, for every
\mathtt{R}_{i}
, for
0\leq i\leq h
, we build the

\theta_{i}
such that

\theta_{i}\;\triangleright\;\vdash\left\{\psi_{i}^{h}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{R}_{i}\;\left\{\psi_{i+1}^{h}\right\}

These trees are descried in
Figure

11
and can be combined by means of multiple applications of the
\mathsf{Seq}
rule, this produces as conclusion the formula
{\psi_{h+1}^{h}}
, i.e.
{\mathbf{U}(s_{h+1}){}}
.

All these trees can
be combined with the
\mathsf{Seq}
rule to prove the triple

\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{0}))^{s_{0}:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\left({\Sep_{j=i}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{R}_{0};\ldots;\mathtt{R}_{h}\;\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{h+1}))^{\Delta_{h}}}\right\}

This triple is obtained with multiple applications of the
\mathsf{Seq}
rule
to the family of trees
\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{h}
.
The claim is shown with of an application of the

\mathsf{Seq}
rule to the last triple and
(*)
.

The tree
\pi_{i}
is the following one:

\left\{{({(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\;\;\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}})}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{P}_{i}\;\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\left\{{({(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\;\;\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}})}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{P}_{i}\;\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i})\land\mathbf{IS}(b_{i},\mathit{head}(r_{i}))\land\mathbf{IS}(k,\mathit{tail}(r_{i})))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\pi_{i}^{1}\pi_{i}^{2}\pi_{i}^{3}\text{and \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rem:conjasscomm}}\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:extcompcomp}}\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:prekm}}\eqref{eq:axsplit}

The trees
\pi_{i}^{1},\pi_{i}^{2},\pi_{i}^{3}
are respectively the following ones:

\left\{{({(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\;\;\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}})}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash r_{i}\leftarrow g(k)\;\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i}))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1]}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\left\{{({(\mathbf{U}(k))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]}}*\;\;\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}})}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash r_{i}\leftarrow g(k)\;\left\{{{({\mathbf{U}(k)}\land\mathbf{IS}(r_{i},g(k)))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1]}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and k:\mathsf{Str}[n]\vdash g(k):\mathsf{Str}[n+1]r_{i}\notin\mathsf{FV}(g(k)){\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\in\Phi r_{i}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\{k:\mathsf{Str}[n]\})\text{\eqref{eq:axpotp}, (W1), (W2) and (U1)}

\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i}))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1]}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash b_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{head}(r_{i})\;\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i})\land\mathbf{IS}(b_{i},\mathit{head}(r_{i})))^{r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i}))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1]}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash b_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{head}(r_{i})\;\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i})\land\mathbf{IS}(b_{i},\mathit{head}(r_{i})))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1]\vdash\mathit{head}(r_{i}):\mathbb{B}b_{i}\notin\mathsf{FV}(\mathit{head}(r_{i}))\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\in\Phi b_{i}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1]\})

\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i}))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1]}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash k\leftarrow\mathit{tail}(r_{i})\;\left\{{{(\mathbf{U}(r_{i})\land\mathbf{IS}(b_{i},\mathit{head}(r_{i}))\land\mathbf{IS}(k,\mathit{tail}(r_{i})))^{k:\mathsf{Str}[n],r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}}}*\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}}\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}\vdash\mathit{tail}(r_{i}):\mathsf{Str}[n]k\notin\mathsf{FV}(\mathit{tail}(r_{i}))\Sep_{j=0}^{i-1}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\in\Phi k\notin\mathsf{dom}(\{r_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+1],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}\})

Figure 10:
Construction of the family of trees
\pi_{i}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{i}))^{s_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i]}}*\left({\Sep_{j=i}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{R}_{i}\;\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{i+1}))^{s_{i+1}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i+1]}}*\left({\Sep_{j=i+1}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{i})*\mathbf{U}(b_{i}))^{s_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}}}*\left({\Sep_{j={i+1}}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{R}_{i}\;\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{i+1}))^{s_{i+1}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i+1]}}*\left({\Sep_{j=i+1}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{i})*\mathbf{U}(b_{i}))^{s_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}}}*\left({\Sep_{j={i+1}}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}\;\Delta_{h}\vdash\mathtt{R}_{i}\;\left\{{(\mathbf{U}(s_{i})*\mathbf{U}(b_{i})\land\mathbf{IS}(s_{i+1},\mathit{concat}({s_{i}},{b_{i}})))^{s_{i+1}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i+1],s_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}}}*\left({\Sep_{j=i+1}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\right)\right\}\lx@proof@logical@and s_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}\vdash\mathit{concat}({s_{i}},{b_{i}}):\mathsf{Str}[n+i+1]s_{n+i+1}\notin\mathsf{FV}({\mathit{concat}({s_{i}},{b_{i}})}){\Sep_{j={i+1}}^{h}}\;\;{(\mathbf{U}(b_{j}))^{b_{j}:\mathsf{Bool}}}\in\Phi s_{i+1}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\{s_{i}:\mathsf{Str}[n+i],b_{i}:\mathbb{B}\})\text{ and \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:prekm}.}\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:extcompcomp}}\text{\eqref{eq:axmerge}}\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rem:conjasscomm}}

Figure 11:
Construction of the family of trees
\theta_{i}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

7
Characterizing Cryptographic Security Through Computational Independence

The study of computational independence and its cryptographic applications
originates with
[
15
]
2
2

2

The approach of
[
15
]
was
motivated by Yao’s characterization of encryption security using a
computational notion of mutual information
[
3
]
, which we take as our
starting point. We note however, that the definition given there does not
directly coincide with the notion that we use to interpret separating
conjunction in CSL. In this section, we will give a definition that is more
closely related to the approach of this paper, and show that with respect to
efficiently samplable distributions it is indeed equivalent to that given in

[
15
]
. Then we use this definition to give a characterization of
encryption security in terms of computational independence. In this way,
we show that the forms
of logical and structural reasoning provided by CSL are consistent
with the standard approach of provable security in computational cryptography.

7.1
Defining and Characterizing Computational Independence

We begin by recalling some basic definitions for this setting.

Definition 12
.

A
random variable

\mathbf{R}
over a finite set
X
denotes a random process taking values in
X
according to some distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R}}
. We write
\Pr[\mathbf{R}=x]
to denote
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R}}(x)
. Given a family
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
of finite sets, a
random variable ensemble
is a family
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
, where
\mathbf{R}_{n}
is a random variable over
X_{n}
. Let
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{Q}
be jointly distributed random variables over
X,Y
.
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{Q}
are
(statistically) independent
if their joint distribution function satisfies:
\Pr[\mathbf{R}=x\wedge\mathbf{Q}=y]=\Pr[\mathbf{R}=x]\cdot\Pr[\mathbf{R}=y]
. The joint distribution function
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{Q}}
of
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{Q}
induces
marginal distribution functions:

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R}}
is defined by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R}}(x)=\sum_{y\in Y}\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{Q}}(x,y)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Q}}
is defined similarly. We write
\mathbf{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathbf{Q}
to denote the random variable obtained by sampling pairs of values independently from the random variables with these respective marginal distribution functions.
We note that these definitions may be generalized in the natural way from pairs
of random variables to arbitrary tuples, and also lift to random variable
ensembles. A random variable is
efficiently samplable
if its
distribution function is efficiently samplable. Random variable ensembles

\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
where
\mathbf{R}_{n}
has
distribution
\mathcal{D}_{n}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{n}
has distribution
\mathcal{E}_{n}
are

computationally indistinguishable
if
\{\mathcal{D}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\approx\{\mathcal{E}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
. In this case we write

\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}\approx\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
.
∎

To simplify notation, we will write
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
as shorthand for

\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}_{n\in\omega}
, and write
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\},...
to denote random variable ensembles. We note that the notation
\mathrel{\otimes}
is
consistent with that introduced for distributions. In particular, it is not
hard to verify that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathbf{Q}}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{R}}\mathrel{\otimes}\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Q}}
. What we are calling a random variable (ensemble)
is often called a distribution (ensemble). Indeed, in the discrete setting,
the notions of distribution, random variable, and probability mass function are
often used synonymously. Here we are being more careful.

It is now time to rephrase one of the key concepts of this paper in terms of
random variables:

Definition 13
.

\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
are
computationally independent
if there exist statistically independent ensembles
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
such that
(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})\approx(\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\})
.
∎

This definition, from
[
15
]
, was originally given with respect to both uniform and
nonuniform adversaries. We restrict attention to uniform adversaries. Moreover,
unless otherwise noted we will assume that all random variable ensembles are
efficiently samplable. This includes the witnessing ensembles in the definition
of computational independence.

The alternate characterization mentioned above is given by the following
result, which states that computational independence can be characterized,
again through indistinguishability, but referring to tensor products, instead.

Theorem 1
.

\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
are computationally independent if and only if
(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})\approx\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
.
∎

First of all, one can observe that efficient
samplability is preserved by taking projections:

Proposition 6
.

Suppose that jointly distributed
(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})
are efficiently samplable.
Then
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
are efficiently samplable.
∎

Then, one can easily prove that indistinguishability between the
corresponding
components
marginals of a product random variable follows from the indistinguishability
between the two products themselves:

Lemma 31
.

For any (not necessarily efficiently samplable)

\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
,
if
(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})\approx(\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\})
, then
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
.
∎

Proof.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a probabilistic poly-time (PPT) distinguisher that distinguishes
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
with advantage
\epsilon(n)
and define the distinguisher
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
by
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x,y)={\mathcal{A}}(x)
. Then
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is a PPT distinguisher that distinguishes
(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})
and
(\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\})
with advantage
\epsilon(n)
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

Suppose
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
, are ensembles such that
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
. Then
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\not\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
, as witnessed by the PPT distinguisher
{\mathcal{A}}
with advantage
\epsilon(n)
Let
\mathcal{S}
be a PPT sampler for
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
. Define
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
by

{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(x)={\mathcal{A}}(x,\mathcal{S}(1^{|x|}))
. Then
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is PPT, and it distinguishes
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
from
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
with advantage
\epsilon(n)
.
∎

Using
Lemma

32
, we can show that two pairs of indistinguishable random
variables ensembles can be combined through tensor
product preserving indistinguishability:

Lemma 33
.

If
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
and

\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
, then
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
.
∎

Proof.

By Lemma
32
we have
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
and, by a similar argument,
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
. The desired result follows by the transitivity of
\approx
.
∎

Theorem

1
follows naturally from these intermediate
results.

Proof.

Suppose
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
are computationally independent, as witnessed by statistically independent distributions
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
.
By Lemma
31
,
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
and then by Lemma
33
,
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
. By the independence of
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
, we have that
(\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\})
and
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
are identically distributed, so
a fortiori

(\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\})\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
. This gives

\displaystyle(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})\approx(\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\})\approx\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}

For the converse, note that by Proposition
6
the marginal distributions
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
are efficiently samplable, so just take independently sampled copies
\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}_{n}\}
of
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
, respectively.
∎

In the following, we take Theorem
1
as the characterization
of computational independence, and introduce the following notation:
we write
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\perp\!\!\!\perp_{c}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
to denote

(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})\approx\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\mathrel{\otimes}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
.

7.2
Computational Independence in a Cryptographic Setting

We now give two examples of the use of computational independence in a cryptographic setting. The first proves a new fundamental property of pseudorandom random variable ensembles. The second is a characterization of encryption security against eavesdropping adversaries. Such a characterization was provided for nonuniform adversaries in
[
15
]
. Here we extend the characterization to the uniform setting.

Write
\mathbf{U}(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\})
to denote that
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
is pseudorandom (i.e.
indistinguishable from the uniform random variable ensemble.) The following result
tells us that a joint random variable ensemble being pseudorandom is equivalent
to their components being computationally independent
and
pseudorandom.

Lemma 34
.

Suppose
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}
,
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
are samplable ensembles. Then
\mathbf{U}((\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}))
if and only if
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\perp\!\!\!\perp_{c}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{U}(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\})
,
\mathbf{U}(\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})
.
∎

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{U}(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\})
,
\mathbf{U}(\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})
and
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\perp\!\!\!\perp_{c}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
. So we have
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{U}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}\approx\{\mathbf{V}_{n}\}
where
\mathbf{U}_{n}
,
\mathbf{V}_{n}
are uniform. It then follows by Lemma
33
that
\mathbf{U}(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\otimes\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})
. From
\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\}\perp\!\!\!\perp_{c}\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}
we may conclude that

\mathbf{U}((\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}))
.
Conversely, if
\mathbf{U}((\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\}))
then
(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})\approx\{\mathbf{W}_{n}\}
where
\mathbf{W}_{n}
is uniform.
But then
(\{\mathbf{R}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{Q}_{n}\})\approx(\{\mathbf{U}_{n}\},\{\mathbf{V}_{n}\})
where
\mathbf{U}_{n}
,
